# Hamas

For other uses, see [Hamas (disambiguation)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hamas_\(disambiguation\)).

Not to be confused with [Hamaas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hamaas).

"Islamic Resistance Movement" redirects here. For other uses, see [Islamic Resistance (disambiguation)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_Resistance_\(disambiguation\)).

"Harakat al-Muqawama al-Islamiya" redirects here. Not to be confused with [Abu Sayyaf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abu_Sayyaf) (Al Hamas Harakat Al Muqawamah Al Islamiyyah).

The **Islamic Resistance Movement**, abbreviated **Hamas**[^cite_note-26] (an acronym from the [Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic_language): حركة المقاومة الإسلامية, [romanized](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romanization_of_Arabic): *Ḥarakat al-Muqāwamah al-ʾIslāmiyyah*),[^cite_note-\:8-27][^cite_note-28] is a [Sunni](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sunni) [Islamist](15012.md) [Palestinian nationalist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestinian_nationalist) political organisation with a military wing known as the [al-Qassam Brigades](1703333.md). It has [governed](38626632.md) the [Israeli-occupied Gaza Strip](76465951.md) since 2007.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKear201822-29][^cite_note-\:9-30] In July 2026, it announced the resignation of its civilian administration under the [Gaza peace plan](81215079.md) and US President [Donald Trump](4848272.md) announced that his [Board of Peace](81274522.md) had reached an agreement to disarm Hamas.[^cite_note-GazaGovtResigns-31][^cite_note-HurdlesAndUncertainty-32]&#x20;

The Hamas movement was founded by Palestinian Islamic scholar [Ahmed Yassin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ahmed_Yassin) in 1987 after the outbreak of the [First Intifada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Intifada) against the Israeli occupation. It emerged from his 1973 [Mujama al-Islamiya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mujama_al-Islamiya) Islamic charity affiliated with the [Muslim Brotherhood](20742.md).[^cite_note-\:10-33] Initially, Hamas was discreetly supported by [Israel](9282173.md), as a counter-balance to the secular [Palestine Liberation Organization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestine_Liberation_Organization) (PLO) to prevent the creation of an independent Palestinian state.[^cite_note-Khalidi-34][^cite_note-TOI123-35] In the 2006 [Palestinian legislative election](38945340.md), Hamas secured a majority in the [Palestinian Legislative Council](40067464.md) by campaigning on promises of a corruption-free government and advocating for resistance as a means to liberate Palestine from Israeli occupation.[^cite_note-\:11-36][^cite_note-37] In the 2007 [Battle of Gaza](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Gaza_\(2007\)), Hamas seized control of the Gaza Strip from rival Palestinian faction [Fatah](11104.md).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDavis201767–69-38][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMukhimer2012vii,_58-39] Hamas has since governed the territory separately from the [Palestinian National Authority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestinian_National_Authority), and many countries and nongovernmental organizations have criticized it for human rights violations ([Criticism of Hamas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Criticism_of_Hamas)).[^cite_note-\:12-40] After Hamas's takeover, Israel significantly intensified existing movement restrictions and imposed a complete [blockade of the Gaza Strip](19224310.md).[^cite_note-41] Egypt also began its blockade of Gaza at this time. This was followed by multiple wars with Israel, including those [in 2008–09](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gaza_War_\(2008%E2%80%932009\)), [2012](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2012_Gaza_War), [2014](43243199.md), [2021](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_Israel%E2%80%93Palestine_crisis), and [an ongoing one since 2023](74998519.md), which began with the [October 7 attacks](74998317.md).&#x20;

Hamas has promoted Palestinian nationalism in an [Islamic context](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religious_nationalism)[^cite_note-42] and initially sought a state in all of former [Mandatory Palestine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mandatory_Palestine). It began acquiescing to 1967 borders in the agreements it signed with Fatah in [2005](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestinian_Cairo_Declaration), [2006](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestinian_Prisoners%27_Document) and [2007](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fatah%E2%80%93Hamas_Mecca_Agreement).[^cite_note-seurat1719-43][^cite_note-prisoners-44][^cite_note-cairo-45] In 2017, Hamas released a [new charter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2017_Hamas_charter)[^cite_note-Jazeera,2May2017-46] that supported a Palestinian state within the 1967 borders without recognizing Israel.[^cite_note-borders1967-47][^cite_note-48][^cite_note-49] Hamas's repeated offers of a [truce](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hudna) (for a period of 10–100 years[^cite_note-atran-50]<sup>: 221–246 </sup>) based on the 1967 borders are seen by many as consistent with a [two-state solution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Two-state_solution),[^cite_note-Jihad-51][^cite_note-Baconi-108-52] while others state that Hamas retains the long-term objective of establishing one state in former Mandatory Palestine.[^cite_note-Alsoos2-53]<sup>: 837, 839 </sup> While the [1988 Hamas charter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1988_Hamas_charter) was widely described as [antisemitic](1078.md),[^cite_note-Hamed2023-54] Hamas's 2017 charter[^cite_note-55] removed the antisemitic language and declared [Zionists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zionists), not Jews, the targets of their struggle.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESeurat201917-56][^cite_note-Haaretz20170503-57][^cite_note-Spitka2023-58][^cite_note-AlJazeera20170506-59][^cite_note-Hroub-60] It has been debated whether the charter has reflected an actual change in policy.[^cite_note-Spoerl_2020_pp._210–244-61]&#x20;

In terms of foreign policy, Hamas has historically sought out relations with Egypt,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESeurat202288-62] Iran,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESeurat202288-62] Qatar,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBaconi2018181-63] Saudi Arabia,[^cite_note-CarnegieEndowment20150901-64] Syria[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESeurat202288-62] and Turkey;[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESeurat2022254-65] some of its relations have been impacted by the [Arab Spring](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_Spring).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESeurat2022115,_214-66] Hamas and Israel have engaged in the protracted [Gaza–Israel conflict](16346266.md), as part of the broader [Israeli–Palestinian conflict](46216.md). Hamas has attacked Israeli civilians, including through [suicide bombings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestinian_suicide_attacks) as well as [launching rockets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestinian_rocket_attacks_on_Israel) at Israeli cities. Australia, Canada, Costa Rica, Ecuador, Honduras, Israel, Japan, New Zealand, Paraguay, Trinidad and Tobago, the United Kingdom, the United States, and the European Union have designated Hamas as a [terrorist organisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Terrorist_organisation). In 2018 and 2023, motions at the [United Nations](31769.md) to condemn Hamas as a terrorist organization failed to meet the two-thirds threshold.[^cite_note-68][^cite_note-69][^cite_note-70]&#x20;

## Etymology

*Hamas* is an [acronym](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Acronym) of the [Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic) phrase حركة المقاومة الإسلامية or *Ḥarakah al-Muqāwamah al-ʾIslāmiyyah*, meaning "Islamic Resistance Movement". This acronym, HMS, was [glossed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossed) in the [1988 Hamas Covenant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1988_Hamas_charter)[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJefferis2016119-71] by the Arabic word *ḥamās* (حماس) which itself means "zeal", "strength", or "bravery".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHerzog200684-72]&#x20;

## History

Main article: [History of Hamas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Hamas)

Hamas was established in 1987, and allegedly has its origins in [Egypt](8087628.md)'s [Muslim Brotherhood](20742.md) movement,[^cite_note-73][^cite_note-74] which had been active in the [Gaza Strip](12047.md) since the 1950s and gained influence through a network of mosques and various charitable and social organizations. Unlike other Palestinian factions, after the [Israeli occupation of Gaza in 1967](29328.md), the Brotherhood in Gaza refused to join the resistance boycott against Israel.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFiliu201255-75]&#x20;

In the 1980s, Hamas emerged as a powerful political factor, challenging the influence of the [PLO](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PLO), whose [Fatah](11104.md) faction it had played a core role in creating.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFiliu201255-75] In December 1987, the Brotherhood adopted a more nationalist and activist line under the name of Hamas.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFiliu201266-76]&#x20;

Hamas was initially discreetly supported by Israel as a counter-balance to the secular [PLO](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PLO).[^cite_note-Khalidi-34] During the 1990s and early 2000s, the organization conducted numerous suicide bombings and other attacks against Israel.[^cite_note-Kimmerling-77]&#x20;

In the Palestinian legislative election of January 2006, Hamas gained a large majority of seats in the [Palestinian Parliament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestinian_Parliament), defeating the ruling [Fatah](11104.md) party. After the elections, conflicts arose between Hamas and Fatah, which they were unable to resolve.[^cite_note-78][^cite_note-canadafreepress.com-79] In June 2007, Hamas defeated Fatah in a [series of violent clashes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fatah-Hamas_conflict#Conflict), and since that time Hamas has governed the [Gaza](12047.md) portion of the [Palestinian territories](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestinian_territories), while at the same time they were ousted from government positions in the [West Bank](33209.md).[^cite_note-BBC-Hamas-who-80][^cite_note-81] [Israel](9282173.md) and [Egypt](8087628.md) then imposed an [economic blockade on Gaza](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_blockade_on_Gaza) and largely sealed their borders with the territory.[^cite_note-iht.com-82][^cite_note-washingtonpost.com-83]&#x20;

After acquiring control of Gaza, Hamas-affiliated and other militias launched rocket attacks upon Israel, which Hamas ceased in June 2008 following an [Egyptian](8087628.md)-brokered ceasefire.[^cite_note-ITIC-84] The ceasefire broke down late in 2008, with each side accusing the other of responsibility.[^cite_note-ynetnews.com-85] In late December 2008, [Israel attacked Gaza](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2008%E2%80%932009_Israel%E2%80%93Gaza_conflict),[^cite_note-86] withdrawing its forces in mid-January 2009.[^cite_note-87] Since 2009, Hamas has faced multiple military confrontations with Israel, notably the 2012 and 2014 Gaza Wars, leading to substantial casualties. Hamas has maintained control over Gaza, often clashing with the Palestinian Authority led by Fatah. Efforts at reconciliation between Hamas and Fatah have seen limited success. Hamas continued to face international isolation and blockades, while engaging in sporadic rocket attacks and tunnel construction activities against Israel.&#x20;

On 7 October 2023, Hamas and other Palestinian militants [attacked](74998317.md) Israel killing nearly 1,200 Israelis, about two thirds of them civilians.[^cite_note-F24231215-88] Approximately 250 Israeli civilians and soldiers were [taken hostage](75025451.md) and brought to the Gaza Strip,[^cite_note-89] with the aim of securing the release of [Palestinian prisoners in Israel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestinian_prisoners_in_Israel) (as part of a prisoner swap).[^cite_note-90] Hamas said its attack was in response to Israel's [continued occupation](2781576.md), [blockade of Gaza](19224310.md), and [settlements expansion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_law_and_Israeli_settlements), as well as alleged [threats](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Aqsa_is_in_danger) to the [Al-Aqsa Mosque](65759.md) and the plight of Palestinians.[^cite_note-aj7oct-invasion-91] There are also reports of [sexual violence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sexual_and_gender-based_violence_in_the_7_October_attack_on_Israel) by Hamas militants, allegations that Hamas has denied.[^cite_note-92] Israel responded by [invading the Gaza Strip](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israeli_invasion_of_the_Gaza_Strip_\(2023%E2%80%93present\)), killing over 70,000 Palestinians, 59.1% of them women, children and the elderly according to a peer-reviewed study in *[The Lancet](463899.md)*.[^cite_note-JamaluddineAbukmailAlyCampbellChecchi2025-93]&#x20;

In 2024, the International Criminal Court issued arrest warrants for three Hamas leaders, Yahya Sinwar, Ismail Haniyeh and Mohammed Deif, for their roles in the Oct. 7 killings in Israel. However, the court dropped the legal proceedings against all three men after Israel killed them, as detailed below. The court has also issued warrants for several Israeli leaders, for alleged war crimes and crimes against humanity for the activities in prosecuting the Israel-Hamas war.[^cite_note-94] [^cite_note-95] On 31 July 2024, [Ismail Haniyeh](41207463.md) was [assassinated](77478235.md) in [Tehran](57654.md), after attending the inauguration ceremony of Iranian president [Masoud Pezeshkian](2650383.md).[^cite_note-reuters31july-96] In August 2024, [Yahya Sinwar](53195047.md), the leader of Hamas in Gaza, was elected chairman of the group, replacing Haniyeh. Per Hamas officials, he was elected due to his considerable popularity in the [Arab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_world) and [Islamic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_world) worlds following the [7 October attacks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/7_October_attacks) and his strong connections with Iran and the "[Axis of Resistance](36658251.md)," an informal Iranian-led political and military coalition.[^cite_note-97][^cite_note-98] On 16 October 2024, IDF troops in southern Rafah on a routine patrol [killed](78140031.md) Sinwar in a chance encounter.[^cite_note-BBC_2024-10-17-99] In January 2025, the *[Wall Street Journal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wall_Street_Journal)*, citing Israeli sources, reported that Sinwar's younger brother, [Mohammed Sinwar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohammed_Sinwar) (aka *Shadow*), was leading Hamas. Its sources said that Israel were "working hard to find him" and that he (aka *Shadow*) along with Izz al-Din Haddad, of al-Qassam Brigades, were the two most senior commanders in the Gaza Strip.[^cite_note-wsj20250113-100] On 19 January 2025, [a ceasefire between Hamas and Israel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/January_2025_Gaza_war_ceasefire) went into effect.&#x20;

In January 2025, Hamas confirmed that its senior military chief, [Mohammed Deif](92534.md), was killed by Israel's military in July 2024.[^cite_note-101] On 18 March 2025, Israel broke the ceasefire and shelling of the territory continued.[^cite_note-102][^cite_note-103] In March 2025, Ismail Barhoum, a member of Hamas's political bureau was killed in an Israeli attack on [Nasser Hospital](43293381.md) in [Khan Younis](101330.md).[^cite_note-104] In April 2025, according to the IDF, Yahya Fathi Abd al-Qader Abu Shaar, the head of Hamas' weapons smuggling network, was killed by the Israeli army.[^cite_note-105] In May 2025, there were unconfirmed reports that [Mohammed Sinwar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohammed_Sinwar), Hamas leader in Gaza and the brother of Yahya Sinwar, and [Muhammad Shabana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad_Shabana), commander of the Rafah Brigade, were killed by Israeli airstrike in the southern Gaza Strip.[^cite_note-106][^cite_note-107] In June 2025, the Israeli military confirmed that it had identified the body of Mohammed Sinwar through [DNA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/DNA) checks.[^cite_note-108] In August 2025, [Abu Obaida](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abu_Obaida), the spokesman for Hamas's armed wing, was killed in an Israeli aerial [attack](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assassination_of_Abu_Obaida).[^cite_note-109] A third ceasefire came into effect on 10 October after Israel and Hamas agreed to phase one of a [US-backed peace plan](81215079.md).[^cite_note-theguardian20251009-110][^cite_note-wsj20251010-111] On 6 July 2026, Hamas announced the resignation of [its civil administration](38626632.md) under the [Gaza peace plan](81215079.md).[^cite_note-GazaGovtResigns-31][^cite_note-112][^cite_note-113]&#x20;

On 31 July 2026, Trump said that his [Board of Peace](81274522.md) had reached an agreement to disarm Hamas and [other militant organisations in Gaza](75192468.md). Hamas leader [Ghazi Hamad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ghazi_Hamad) declared that "draft agreement" announced by Trump calls for the transfer of heavy weaponry in Gaza to a Palestinian administration following Israeli withdrawal from Gaza.[^cite_note-114][^cite_note-HurdlesAndUncertainty-32]&#x20;

Hamas' policy towards Israel has evolved. Historically, Hamas envisioned a Palestinian state on all of [the territory that belonged to the British Mandate for Palestine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mandatory_Palestine) (that is, from the [Jordan River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jordan_River) to the [Mediterranean Sea](19006.md)),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEO'Malley2015118-115] and in its [1988 Charter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1988_Hamas_charter) framed the Mandate Palestine as 'Islamic [waqf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Waqf)' or endowment, under sovereignty of [God](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/God_in_Islam).[^cite_note-Alsoos2-53]<sup>: 837, 839 </sup> Hamas leaders have repeatedly emphasized they do not recognize Israel.[^cite_note-Jazeera,2May2017-46] Since then the movement has on several occasions accepted the 1967 borders in formal agreement (in 2005,[^cite_note-cairo-45] 2006,[^cite_note-seurat1719-43] and 2007[^cite_note-kear-217-116]) and in its 2017 charter.[^cite_note-govtandpolitics-117] Some scholars interpret this acceptance of the 1967 borders as an implicit acknowledgement of another political entity on the opposite side,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrenner2022206-118][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEZartman2020230-119][^cite_note-govtandpolitics-117] while others argue that Hamas still maintains the long-term goal of establishing a single state across the territory of the former Mandatory Palestine.[^cite_note-Alsoos2-53]<sup>: 837, 839 </sup>[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMishalSela2006109–110-120][^cite_note-\:0-121][^cite_note-\:1-122][^cite_note-\:2-123] There is ongoing debate about whether Hamas would formally recognize Israel as part of a future peace agreement.[^cite_note-\:3-124][^cite_note-\:4-125][^cite_note-\:5-126] Some analysts suggest that the continued refusal to officially recognize Israel may function primarily as leverage in potential negotiations.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBaconi2018230-127] Some scholars have also drawn parallels between Hamas’s position on recognizing Israel and the historical stance of Likud, which has not recognized a Palestinian state in the West Bank and Gaza.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBeinart2012219,_n.53-128][^cite_note-Emmett-129][^cite_note-Chomsky2010-130][^cite_note-\:6-131][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBaconi2018230-127]&#x20;

### Truce proposals

The founder of Hamas, sheikh [Ahmed Yassin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ahmed_Yassin), has offered Israel a ten-year *[hudna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hudna)* ([armistice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Armistice)) in return for complete Israeli withdrawal from the territories captured in the 1967 war and establishing a Palestinian state in West Bank and Gaza. Later, Yassin has stated that this *hudna* could be renewed for 30, 40 or even 100 years.[^cite_note-atran-50]<sup>: 238 </sup>[^cite_note-fiftyyears-132] Hamas's spokesperson, [Ahmed Yousef](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ahmed_Yousef), has said that a "hudna" is more than a ceasefire and it "obliges parties to use the period to seek a permanent, non-violent resolution to their differences."[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDunning2016179-133] Under [Islamic international law](28840.md), a hudna is a binding and the [Qur'an](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qur%27an) prohibits its violation.[^cite_note-tuastad2-134]&#x20;

Hamas first proposed a *hudna* in 1999. In exchange Israel would have to end the occupation of West Bank and Gaza Strip and release all [Palestinian prisoners](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestinian_prisoners).[^cite_note-tuastad2-134] The 1999 proposal did not mention the issues of the return of the Palestinian refugees or Hamas's recognition of Israel, but interviewed Hamas leaders in 2010 added that the [right of return](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestinian_right_of_return) should be accepted in principle by Israel (without direct actual implementation). Israel and Hamas should use the period of calm (armistice) for negotiating these two issues; if they would also be settled the temporary peace would convert into a permanent peace agreement.[^cite_note-tuastad2-134]&#x20;

In 2006, Ismail Haniyeh, shortly after being [elected](38945340.md) as [Prime Minister](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prime_Minister_of_the_Palestinian_National_Authority), sent messages both to US President [George W. Bush](3414021.md) and to Israel's leaders, offering a long-term truce. Neither Israel nor the United States responded.[^cite_note-Kamel-135] Haniyeh's proposal reportedly was a fifty-year armistice with Israel, if a Palestinian state is created along the 1967 borders with East Jerusalem as its capital.[^cite_note-fiftyyears-132] A Hamas official added that the armistice would renew automatically each time.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESlater2020285-136] In mid-2006, [University of Maryland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Maryland)'s Jerome Segal suggested that a Palestinian state within the 1967 borders and a truce for many years could be considered Hamas's *de facto* recognition of Israel.[^cite_note-Hatz_14Aug2008-137] A similar proposal was once again offered by Hamas to Israel in November 2006.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrenner202236-138]&#x20;

In November 2008, in a meeting, on Gaza Strip soil, with 11 [European](9239.md) members of parliaments, Hamas leader Ismail Haniyeh re-stated that Hamas was willing to accept a Palestinian state "in [the territories of 1967](29328.md)" ([Gaza Strip](12047.md) and [West Bank](33209.md)), and offered Israel a long-term truce if Israel recognized the [Palestinians' national rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestinian_right_of_return); and stated that Israel rejected this proposal.[^cite_note-offer_2008-139] A Hamas finance minister around 2018 contended that such a "long-term ceasefire as understood by Hamas and a two-state settlement are the same".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBaconi2018108-140]&#x20;

According to Leila Seurat, the movement's leaders consider that a traditional peace treaty like those in the Western tradition would be tantamount to surrender, while a truce would provide an alternative allowing them to wait for an inversion in the regional balance of power to the Palestinians' advantage.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESeurat202215-141] Abusada, a political scientist at [Al Azhar University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Azhar_University_%E2%80%93_Gaza), wrote in 2008 that Hamas talks "of hudna \[temporary ceasefire], not of peace or reconciliation with Israel. They believe over time they will be strong enough to liberate all historic Palestine."[^cite_note-erlanger-142] Some scholars have noted that alongside offering a long-term truce, Hamas retains its objective of establishing one state in former [Mandatory Palestine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mandatory_Palestine).[^cite_note-Alsoos2-53]<sup>: 837, 839 </sup> Hamas originally proposed a 10-year truce, or *[hudna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hudna)*, to Israel, contingent on the creation of a Palestinian state based on the 1967 borders. Sheikh Ahmed Yassin indicated that such truce could be extended for 30, 40, or even 100 years, but it would never signal a recognition of Israel. A Hamas official said that having an indefinite truce with Israel doesn't contradict Hamas's lack of recognition of Israel, comparing it to the [Irish Republican Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irish_Republican_Army)'s willingness to accept a permanent armistice with the [United Kingdom](31717.md) without recognizing the UK's sovereignty over Northern Ireland.[^cite_note-atran-50]<sup>: 221–246 </sup>&#x20;

### Recognition of Israel

Hamas leaders have repeatedly emphasized they do not recognize Israel.[^cite_note-Jazeera,2May2017-46] But Hamas has also repeatedly accepted the 1967 borders in signed agreements (in 2005,[^cite_note-cairo-45] 2006,[^cite_note-seurat1719-43] and 2007[^cite_note-kear-217-116]) and in its 2017 charter.[^cite_note-govtandpolitics-117] Some scholars saw Hamas' acceptance of the 1967 borders as a tacit acceptance of another entity on the other side,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrenner2022206-118][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEZartman2020230-119][^cite_note-govtandpolitics-117] while others state that Hamas retains the long-term objective of establishing one state in former Mandatory Palestine.[^cite_note-Alsoos2-53]<sup>: 837, 839 </sup>[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMishalSela2006109–110-120][^cite_note-\:0-121][^cite_note-\:1-122][^cite_note-\:2-123] Whether Hamas would recognize Israel in a future peace agreement is debated.[^cite_note-\:3-124][^cite_note-\:4-125][^cite_note-\:5-126] Others argue that the long-term objective and lack of official recognition of Israel is merely maintained as a bargaining chip for future negotiations.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBaconi2018230-127] Several scholars have compared Hamas's lack of recognition of Israel to [Likud](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Likud)'s lack of recognition of a Palestinian state in the West Bank and Gaza.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBeinart2012219,_n.53-128][^cite_note-Emmett-129][^cite_note-Chomsky2010-130][^cite_note-\:6-131][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBaconi2018230-127]&#x20;

In 1995 Hamas repeated its rejection of any recognition of Israel.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBaconi201832-143] However, after Hamas won the 2006 elections, it did not implement the 1988 Charter as policy, and instead agreed to work with the existing Palestinian political system.[^cite_note-Alsoos2-53]<sup>: 839 </sup> In the [2007 Mecca agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2007_Mecca_agreement), Hamas agreed to respect previous agreements between Fatah and Israel, including the Oslo Accords in which the PLO recognized Israel.[^cite_note-kear-217-116] Both in the 2007 agreement and in the 2006 [Palestinian Prisoners' Document](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestinian_Prisoners%27_Document), Hamas agreed to a Palestinian state based on the 1967 borders. Scholars see this as "implicit" recognition of Israel because by accepting a Palestinian state limited to the 1967 borders, Hamas acknowledged the existence of an entity on the other side.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEZartman2020230-119][^cite_note-govtandpolitics-117]&#x20;

[Mousa Abu Marzook](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mousa_Abu_Marzook), then the vice-president of Hamas' Political Bureau, explained his party's position in 2011: while Hamas did not recognize Israel as a state, it considered the existence of Israel as "amr waqi" (or fait accompli, meaning something that has happened and cannot be changed).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESeurat202250-144] He called this "de facto recognition" of Israel.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESeurat202250-144] Likewise, [Graham Usher](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Graham_Usher_\(journalist\)) writes that while Hamas does not consider Israel to be legitimate, it has accepted Israel as political reality.[^cite_note-145]&#x20;

In 2017, Hamas once again accepted the 1967 borders in its new charter that "drop\[ped] the call for the destruction of Israel from its manifesto".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrenner2022206-118] While it did not abrogate the old charter, Hamas leaders explained that "The original charter has now become a historical document and part of an earlier stage in our evolution. It will remain in the movement's bookshelf as a record of our past."[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrenner2022206-118] Khaled Mashal stated that the new document reflected "our position for now."[^cite_note-aljazeera-2017-05-02-146]&#x20;

Tareq Baconi, a former analyst at the [International Crisis Group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Crisis_Group) currently at [Al-Shabaka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Shabaka) think-tank, notes that Hamas has said it would accept mutual recognition of Israel in any consensus peace deal approved by other Palestinian parties and the population in a referendum.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBaconi2018230-127] To explain why it withholds formal recognition, Baconi argues that Hamas has learned from the fact that, in the 1993 Oslo Accords, the PLO made a "historic concession" in recognizing Israel on 78% of the land of historic Palestine (along the 1967 borders), but was unable to convince Israel to recognize Palestine on the remaining 22% of the land. Having already recognized Israel, the PLO was unable to use recognition to extract any further concessions from Israel, thus according to Baconi the lesson for Hamas was that you can't negotiate from a position of weakness, and the issue of formal recognition of Israel is kept as bargaining chip for negotiations.[^cite_note-147][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBaconi2018230-127]&#x20;

Some scholars, including Baconi, [Ilan Pappé](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ilan_Papp%C3%A9) and [Noam Chomsky](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Noam_Chomsky), have argued that Hamas has offered more to the Israelis than Israeli major parties including Likud have offered in return to the Palestinians, both with its de facto recognition of the 1967 borders and its pledge to accept the recognition of Israel in any future peace deal that has the consensus and approval of the Palestinian parties and population.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBaconi2018230-127][^cite_note-148][^cite_note-149] [Rashid Khalidi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rashid_Khalidi) said in November 2023: "It is well-established that Israeli major governing parties like Likud have refused to recognize Palestinian statehood under any conditions, the constant references to "Judea and Samaria", and this has only increased in recent times with the Knesset passing a resolution opposing Palestinian statehood."[^cite_note-150]&#x20;

### Evolution of positions

#### 1988–1992 (first charter)

In its [early days](#History), Hamas functioned as a [social-religious](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religion) [charity center](1176679.md). Its members armed themselves for [the ongoing resistance against the](47246228.md#PLO_until_the_First_Intifada_\(1964%E2%80%931988\)) [Israeli occupation](29328.md) of the [Palestinian territories](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestinian_territories), and in August 1988 published their [first charter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1988_Hamas_charter) in which Hamas stated that "Israel" should be "eliminated" through a "clash with the enemies", a "struggle against Zionism" and "conflict with Israel".[^cite_note-Charter-151]<sup>: preamble, art. 14, 15, 32 </sup> They wrote that 'Palestine', that is [all of the territory that belonged to the British Mandate for Palestine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mandatory_Palestine) (that is, from the [Jordan River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jordan_River) to the [Mediterranean Sea](19006.md)),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEO'Malley2015118-115] should be "liberated" from "[Zionism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zionism)"[^cite_note-Charter-151]<sup>: art. 14 </sup> and transformed into an [Islamic](6037917.md) *[Waqf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Waqf)* (Islamic charitable [endowment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Financial_endowment)) in which "followers of all religions can coexist in security and safety".[^cite_note-Yale-152]<sup>: art. 6, 11 </sup>[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDalacoura201267-153] Practically speaking, Hamas is and was at war with Israel's army (later also attacking Israeli civilians) [since the spring of 1989](#First_Intifada), initially as part of the [First Intifada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Intifada), a general protest movement that gradually turned more [riotous](146704.md) and violent.&#x20;

#### 1992–2005

In December 1992, Israeli authorities [deported more than 400 Palestinians, suspected to be members of Hamas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1992_deportation_of_Hamas_members) to Southern Lebanon.[^cite_note-154] In 1999, the Hamas leadership, in a memorandum to European diplomats, proposed a long-term ceasefire with Israel in return for Israeli withdrawal of military troops and civilian illegal settlements from [West Bank](33209.md) and [Gaza Strip](12047.md), release of all [Palestinian prisoners](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestinian_prisoners), and the right of Palestinian self-determination (see also section [Truce proposals](#Truce_proposals)).[^cite_note-tuastad2-134] [Sheikh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sheikh) [Ahmed Yassin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ahmed_Yassin), founder of Hamas, who was [assassinated by Israel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Killing_of_Ahmed_Yassin) in 2004, at unreported date has offered Israel a ten-year *[hudna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hudna)* (truce, armistice) in return for establishment of a Palestinian state in the [West Bank](33209.md) and [Gaza](12047.md). Yassin later added, the *hudna* could be renewed, even for longer periods, but would never signal a recognition of Israel.[^cite_note-atran-50]<sup>: 221–246 </sup>&#x20;

In 2005, Hamas signed the [Palestinian Cairo Declaration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestinian_Cairo_Declaration), which confirms "the right of the Palestinian people to resistance in order to end the occupation, establish a Palestinian state with full sovereignty with Jerusalem as its capital" (etc.), aiming to reconcile several Palestinian factions but not describing specific steps or strategies towards Israel.[^cite_note-155]&#x20;

#### 2006–2007: 1967 borders and a truce

In March 2006, after winning an absolute majority in the [2006 Palestinian legislative elections](38945340.md), Hamas published its government program in which Hamas claimed sovereignty for the [Palestinian territories](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestinian_territories) but did not repeat its claim to all of [mandatory Palestine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mandatory_Palestine), instead declared their willingness to have contacts with Israel "in all mundane affairs: business, trade, health, and labor".[^cite_note-KhaledHroub2006-156] The program further stated: "The question of recognizing Israel is not the jurisdiction of one faction, nor the government, but a decision for the Palestinian people."[^cite_note-nixed-157] Since then until today, spokesmen of Hamas seem to disagree about their attitudes towards Israel, and debates are running as to whether the original 1988 Hamas charter has since March 2006 become obsolete and irrelevant or on the contrary still spells out Hamas's genuine and ultimate goals (see: [1988 Hamas charter, § Relevance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1988_Hamas_charter#Relevance_of_the_charter_to_Hamas'_policy)).&#x20;

The March 2006 Hamas legislative program was further explained on 6 June 2006 by Hamas' MP Riad Mustafa: "Hamas will never recognize Israel", but if a popular Palestinian referendum would endorse a peace agreement including recognition of Israel, "we would of course accept their verdict".[^cite_note-nixed-157]&#x20;

Also on 6 June 2006, [Ismail Haniyeh](41207463.md), senior political leader of Hamas and at that time Prime Minister of the [Palestinian National Authority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestinian_government#PNA_governments), sent a letter to US President [George W. Bush](3414021.md) (via [University of Maryland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Maryland)'s [Jerome Segal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jerome_Segal)), stating: "We are so concerned about stability and security in the area that we don't mind having a Palestinian state in the 1967 borders and offering a truce for many years", and asking Bush for a dialogue with the Hamas government. A similar message he sent to Israel's leaders.[^cite_note-Hatz_14Aug2008-137][^cite_note-Kamel-135] Haniyeh had reportedly proposed a fifty-year armistice.[^cite_note-158] Neither Washington nor Israel replied.[^cite_note-Hatz_14Aug2008-137][^cite_note-Kamel-135] Nuancing [sheikh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sheikh) [Ahmed Yassin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ahmed_Yassin)'s statements before 2004 about a *[hudna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hudna)* (truce) with Israel (see above), Hamas's (former) senior adviser [Ahmed Yousef](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ahmed_Yousef) has said (at unknown date) that a "[hudna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hudna)" (truce, armistice) is more than a ceasefire and "obliges parties to use the period to seek a permanent, non-violent resolution to their differences."[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDunning2016179-133]&#x20;

On 28 June 2006, Hamas signed the [second version of (originally) 'the Palestinians' Prisoners Document'](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestinian_Prisoners%27_Document#Second_version:_%22National_Conciliation_Document%22) which supports the quest for a Palestinian state "on all territories occupied in 1967".[^cite_note-prisoners-44][^cite_note-bbc_abbas_risks_all-159][^cite_note-seurat47-160] This document also recognized the PLO as "the sole legitimate representative of the Palestinian people", and states that "the negotiations" should be conducted by PLO and [President of the Palestinian National Authority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/President_of_the_Palestinian_National_Authority) and eventual agreements must be ratified by either the [Palestinian National Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestinian_National_Council) or a general referendum "held in the homeland and the Diaspora". Leila Seurat also notes that this document "implicitly recognized the June 1967 borders, agreed on the construction of a Palestinian state with Jerusalem as a capital and accepted limitations to the resistance in the territories occupied in 1967", and was produced following consultations with the entire Political Bureau.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESeurat2019199-161]&#x20;

In an August 2006 interview with *[The New York Times](30680.md)*, [Ismail Haniyeh](41207463.md), senior political leader of Hamas and then Prime Minister of the [Palestinian National Authority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestinian_government#PNA_governments), said: "We have no problem with a sovereign Palestinian state over all our lands within the 1967 borders, living in calm."[^cite_note-162] In November 2006, Hamas again proposed a temporary but long lasting truce to Israel in exchange for a Palestinian state in the 1967 borders.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrenner202236-138]&#x20;

In February 2007, Hamas signed the [Fatah–Hamas Mecca Agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fatah%E2%80%93Hamas_Mecca_Agreement), stressing "the importance of national unity as basis for (...) confronting the occupation" and "activate and reform the [PLO](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PLO)", but without further details about how to confront or deal with Israel.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESeurat201949-163] At the time of signing that 2007 agreement, [Mousa Abu Marzook](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mousa_Abu_Marzook), Deputy Chairman of the [Hamas Political Bureau](#Political_Bureau), underlined *his* view of the Hamas position: "I can recognize the presence of Israel as a fait accompli (amr wâqi') or, as the French say, a de facto recognition, but this does not mean that I recognize Israel as a state".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESeurat201950-164] More Hamas leaders, through the years, have made similar statements.[^cite_note-Jazeera,2May2017-46][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBaconi2018230-127]&#x20;

In June 2007, Hamas [ousted the Fatah movement from the Gaza Strip, took control there](12047.md#2007:_Hamas_takeover), and since then Hamas occasionally fired rockets from the Gaza Strip on Israel, purportedly to retaliate Israeli aggression against the people of Gaza.[^cite_note-jazeera,22Apr2008-165]&#x20;

#### 2008–2016

In April 2008, former US President [Jimmy Carter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jimmy_Carter) met with [Khaled Mashal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khaled_Mashal), the recognized Hamas leader since 2004. Mashal said to Carter, Hamas would "accept a Palestinian state on the [1967 borders](29328.md)" and accept the right of Israel "to live as a neighbour" if such a deal would be approved by a referendum among the "Palestinians". Nevertheless, Mashal did not offer a unilateral ceasefire (as Carter had suggested him to do). The US State Department showed utter indifference for Mashal's new stance; Israel's Prime Minister [Ehud Olmert](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ehud_Olmert) even refused to meet with Carter in [Jerusalem](16043.md), not to mention paying attention to the new Hamas stance.[^cite_note-jazeera,22Apr2008-165]&#x20;

On 19 June 2008, Hamas and Israel agreed to a six-month cease-fire,[^cite_note-Ref_2008-166] which Hamas declared finished at 18 December[^cite_note-bjt-tip-point-cross-border-figthing-167] amidst mutual accusations of breaching the agreed conditions.[^cite_note-Ref_2008-166]&#x20;

Meanwhile, in November 2008, in a meeting with 11 [European](9239.md) members of parliaments, Hamas senior official [Ismail Haniyeh](41207463.md) repeated what he had written in June 2006 to U.S. President George W. Bush but with one extra condition: "the Hamas government had agreed to accept a Palestinian state that followed [the 1967 borders](29328.md) and to offer Israel a long-term *[hudna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hudna)* (truce), if Israel recognized the [Palestinians' national rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestinian_right_of_return)" – a proposal which he said Israel had declined.[^cite_note-offer_2008-139]&#x20;

In September 2009, [Ismail Haniyeh](41207463.md), [head of the Hamas government in the Gaza Strip](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Governance_of_the_Gaza_Strip), wrote to UN Secretary General [Ban Ki-moon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ban_Ki-moon) that Hamas would support any steps leading to a Palestinian state on the 1967 borders: "We would never thwart efforts to create an independent Palestinian state with borders \[from] June 4, 1967, with Jerusalem as its capital."[^cite_note-offer_2009-168]&#x20;

In May 2010, [Khaled Mashal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khaled_Mashal), chairman of the [Hamas Political Bureau](#Political_Bureau) (thus Hamas' highest leader), again stated that a state "Israel" living next to "a Palestinian state on the borders of 1967" would be acceptable for Hamas – but only if a referendum among "the Palestinian people" would endorse this arrangement. In November 2010, [Ismail Haniyeh](41207463.md),[^cite_note-170] also proposed a Palestinian state on 1967 borders, though added three further conditions: "resolution of [the issue of refugees](74681.md#Palestinian_views)", "the release of Palestinian prisoners", and "Jerusalem as its capital"; and he made the same reservation as Mashal in May 2010 had made, that a Palestinian referendum needed to endorse this arrangement.[^cite_note-Beinart-171][^cite_note-UWR-172]&#x20;

On 1 December 2010, [Ismail Haniyeh](41207463.md) (senior Hamas leader, see above), in a news conference in [Gaza](55387.md), repeated his November 2010 message: "We accept a Palestinian state on the borders of 1967, with Jerusalem as its capital, the release of Palestinian prisoners, and the resolution of the issue of refugees," but only if such arrangement would be endorsed by "a referendum" held among all Palestinians: in Gaza, West Bank, and the diaspora.[^cite_note-offer_2010-173]&#x20;

In May 2011, Hamas and [Fatah](11104.md) signed an agreement in [Cairo](6293.md), agreeing to form a ('national unity') government and appoint the Ministers "in consensus between them", but it contained no remarks about how to confront or deal with Israel.[^cite_note-174] In February 2012, Hamas and [Fatah](11104.md) signed the [Fatah–Hamas Doha Agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fatah%E2%80%93Hamas_Doha_Agreement), agreeing (again) to form an interim national consensus government, which (again) did not materialize.&#x20;

Still in February 2012, according to the [Palestinian Authority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestinian_National_Authority#Two_PNA_administrations) (either the [Fatah](11104.md) branch in West Bank or the Hamas branch in Gaza), Hamas forswore the use of violence against Israel ("ceasefire", an Israeli news website called it), followed by a few weeks without violence between Hamas and Israel.[^cite_note-IBZ_14Mar2012-175][^cite_note-Tab-176] But violence between Israel and Palestinian militant groups, in the Gaza Strip and southern Israel, also involving Hamas, [would soon resume](16346266.md#Operation_%22Returning_Echo%22).&#x20;

#### 2017–6 Oct 2023 (new charter)

See also: [2017 Hamas charter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2017_Hamas_charter)

On 1 May 2017, after much internal discussion, Hamas and its [Hamas Political Bureau](#Political_Bureau) chief [Khaled Mashal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khaled_Mashal) published "A Document of General Principles and Policies", also known as the [2017 Hamas charter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2017_Hamas_charter). In the new charter Hamas accepts a Palestinian state in the 1967 borders, without recognizing Israel.[^cite_note-177][^cite_note-178] When asked, Hamas leaders explained that "The original charter has now become a historical document and part of an earlier stage in our evolution. It will remain in the movement's bookshelf as a record of our past." Khaled Mashal stated that the new document reflected "our position for now."[^cite_note-aljazeera-2017-05-02-146] However, Hamas fell short of formally repudiating the original 1988 charter.[^cite_note-no-softened-179][^cite_note-FOOTNOTESeurat202262-180] According to some analysts Hamas did not formally revoke the old charter so as to not alienate some of its base members, who it feared might join rival Islamist factions.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESeurat202262-180]&#x20;

Around 2018, a Hamas finance minister has suggested that a "long-term ceasefire as understood by Hamas \[*[hudna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hudna)*] and a two-state settlement are the same".[^cite_note-Baconi-108-52] In 2021 Hamas organized and financed a conference among 250 Gaza citizens about the future management of the State of Palestine following the takeover of Israel which was predicted to come soon. According to the conclusions of the conference, the Jewish Israeli fighters would be killed, while the peaceful individuals could be integrated or be allowed to leave. At the same time the highly skilled and educated would be prevented from leaving.[^cite_note-181][^cite_note-182] In 2020 [Ismail Haniyeh](41207463.md) said in an interview that one of the principles of Hamas was "Palestine [from the sea to the river](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/From_the_sea_to_the_river)."[^cite_note-183] In 2022, Yahya Sinwar cautioned Israelis that Hamas would one day "march through your walls to uproot your regime."[^cite_note-WP_Deeper-184]&#x20;

#### 7 Oct 2023–present



Approximate presence of Hamas militants (blue) on 7–9 October 2023

In the [October 7 attacks](74998317.md), Hamas and associates murdered 767 civilians, killed a further 376 security personnel of the state of [Israel](9282173.md) and abducted 251 people from Israel to the [Gaza Strip](12047.md).[^cite_note-185] [Israel retaliated with warfare in the Gaza Strip](74998519.md), aiming at Hamas militants but also harming much civilian infrastructure and directly killing tens of thousands of civilians, more than 80 percent of casualties.[^cite_note-theguardian20250821-186] A number of conflicting statements since then were made by Hamas senior leaders regarding the Hamas policy towards Israel.&#x20;

On 24 October, [Ghazi Hamad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ghazi_Hamad)—member of the [decision-making Hamas Political Bureau](#Political_Bureau)[^cite_note-Libération-187]—explained the 7 October attack: "Israel is a country that has no place on our land. We must remove that country because it constitutes a security, military and political catastrophe to the Arab and Islamic nation". "We are called a nation of [martyrs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shahid) and we are proud to sacrifice martyrs". Hamad called the creation of the Jewish state "illogical": "(...) We are the victims of the occupation. Therefore, nobody should blame us for the things we do".[^cite_note-188][^cite_note-189]&#x20;

On 1 November 2023, [Ismail Haniyeh](41207463.md), then incumbent highest Hamas leader (but [assassinated by Israel 31 July 2024](41207463.md#assassination)), stated that if Israel agreed to a ceasefire in the [Gaza war](74998519.md), if humanitarian corridors would be opened, and aid would be allowed into Gaza, Hamas would be "ready for political negotiations for a two-state solution with [Jerusalem](16043.md) as the capital of Palestine". Haniyeh also praised the support of movements in [Yemen](350939.md), [Iraq](7515928.md), [Syria](7515849.md) and [Lebanon](17771.md) for the Palestinian struggle.[^cite_note-Haniyeh2023-190]&#x20;



Ruins of [Rafah](584373.md) in January 2025 after Israel's [Rafah offensive](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rafah_offensive)

In January 2024 [Khaled Mashal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khaled_Mashal), a former Hamas leader, slighted "The West" and "the two-state solution", saying "The 1967 borders represent 21% of Palestine, which is practically one fifth of its land, so this cannot be accepted", and adding that "our right in Palestine from the sea to the river" cannot be waived.[^cite_note-mashal2-191] However, he reiterated that Hamas "accepts a state on the 1967 borders with Jerusalem as its capital, with complete independence and with the right of return without recognising the legitimacy of the [Zionist entity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zionist_entity)."[^cite_note-mashal2-191]&#x20;

Hamas Member of Parliament [Khalil al-Hayya](43349601.md), also deputy chairman of the Hamas Political Bureau, told the Associated Press in April 2024 that Hamas is willing to agree to a truce of five years or more with Israel and that it would lay down its weapons and convert into a political party if an independent Palestinian state is established along pre-1967 borders.[^cite_note-Sewell_2024_s696-192] The [Associated Press](18935732.md) considered this a "significant concession", but presumed that [Israel](9282173.md) would not even want to consider this scenario following the October 2023 attack.[^cite_note-Sewell_2024_s696-192]&#x20;

#### Reactions

The vision that Hamas articulated in its [original 1988 charter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1988_Hamas_charter) resembles the vision of certain Zionist groups regarding the same territory, as noted by several authors.[^cite_note-UWR-172][^cite_note-193][^cite_note-194] This may suggest that Hamas's views were inspired by those Zionist perspectives.[^cite_note-195][^cite_note-196]&#x20;

Several (other) authors have interpreted the [1988 Hamas charter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1988_Hamas_charter) as a call for "armed struggle against Israel".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEO'Malley2015118-115]&#x20;

In 2009, Taghreed El-khodary and Ethan Bronner, writing in the [New York Times](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_York_Times), said that Hamas' position is that it doesn't recognize Israel's right to exist, but is willing to accept as a compromise a Palestinian state based on the 1967 borders.[^cite_note-nytimes20090906-197]&#x20;

Writing for *Middle Eastern Studies*, Imad Alsoos says that Hamas has both a short and long-term objective: "The short-term objective aims to establish a Palestinian state in Gaza and the West Bank, while the long-term objective still strives to liberate Mandate Palestine in its entirety.[^cite_note-Alsoos2-53]<sup>: 837, 839 </sup> Establishing a Palestinian state in the West Bank and Gaza (as part of a *hudna* deal) would be Hamas's interim solution, during which Israel would not be recognized.[^cite_note-Alsoos2-53]<sup>: 837, 839 </sup>[^cite_note-198]&#x20;

In mid-2006, [University of Maryland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Maryland)'s [Jerome Segal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jerome_Segal) suggested that a Palestinian state within the 1967 borders and a truce for many years could be considered Hamas's de facto recognition of Israel.[^cite_note-Hatz_14Aug2008-137]&#x20;

Journalist [Zaki Chehab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zaki_Chehab) wrote in 2007 that Hamas's public concessions following the 2006 elections were "window-dressing" and that the organisation would never recognize Israel's right to exist.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEChehab2007203-199]&#x20;

As to the question whether Hamas would be capable to enter into a long-term non-aggression treaty with Israel without being disloyal to their understanding of Islamic law and God's word, *[The Atlantic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Atlantic)* magazine columnist [Jeffrey Goldberg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jeffrey_Goldberg) in January 2009 stated: "I tend to think not, though I've noticed over the years a certain plasticity of belief among some Hamas ideologues. Also, this is the Middle East, so anything is possible".[^cite_note-200]&#x20;

Professor [Mohammed Ayoob](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohammed_Ayoob) in his 2020 book, while discussing the [2017 Hamas charter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2017_Hamas_charter), stated that "acceptance of the 1967 borders can be interpreted as a de facto acceptance of the preconditions for a two-state solution".[^cite_note-201]&#x20;

## Religious policy

### Gaza Strip

Main articles: [Islamism in the Gaza Strip](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamism_in_the_Gaza_Strip), [Islamist anti-Hamas groups in the Gaza Strip](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamist_anti-Hamas_groups_in_the_Gaza_Strip), [Jamila Abdallah Taha al-Shanti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jamila_Abdallah_Taha_al-Shanti), and [Palestinian Christians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestinian_Christians)

Hamas' Change and Reform [electoral list](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electoral_list) for the [2006 Palestinian legislative election](38945340.md) included a [Palestinian Christian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestinian_Christian) candidate, Hosam al-Taweel, running as an independent for the Christian [reserved](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reserved_political_positions) seat in [Gaza City](55387.md).[^cite_note-204][^cite_note-AJ_HT-203][^cite_note-PI_HT-205][^cite_note-API_HT-206] Hosam al-Taweel won the seat, one of six seats reserved for Palestine's Christian minority, with the highest winning vote of the six elected, due to the endorsement of Hamas and other nationalist groups.[^cite_note-imec_ht-207]&#x20;

The gender ideology outlined in the Hamas charter, the importance of women in the religious-nationalist project of liberation is asserted as no lesser than that of males. Their role was defined primarily as one of manufacturing males and caring for their upbringing and rearing, though the charter recognized they could fight for liberation without obtaining their husband's permission and in 2002 their participation in jihad was permitted.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDavis201755-208] The doctrinal emphasis on childbearing and rearing as woman's primary duty is not so different from Fatah's view of women in the First Intifada and it also resembles the outlook of Jewish settlers, and over time it has been subjected to change.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShitrit201574-209] A few were worried about imposition of Islamic dress codes, but most Christians in the Gaza Strip said these worries were baseless and they were not worried about the Hamas government.[^cite_note-imec_ht-207]&#x20;

In 1989, during the First Intifada, a few Hamas followers[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShitrit201574-209] campaigned for [polygamy](24475.md), and insisted women stay at home and be segregated from men. In the course of this campaign, women who chose not to wear the hijab were verbally and physically harassed, with the result that the hijab was being worn 'just to avoid problems on the streets'.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERubenberg2001230–31-210] The harassment dropped drastically when, after 18 months, the [Unified National Leadership of the Uprising](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unified_National_Leadership_of_the_Uprising) (UNLU) condemned it.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGerner200727-211] Polygamy is practised in some [Bedouin](61770.md) communities in Israel, and some Palestinians with Israeli citizenship, particularly in the Negev desert (Arabic pronunciation: Naqab) surrounding the Gaza Strip.[^cite_note-212][^cite_note-213][^cite_note-214][^cite_note-215][^cite_note-216]&#x20;

Since Hamas took control of the Gaza Strip in 2007, some of its members have attempted to impose Islamic dress or the [hijab](68301.md) head covering on women.[^cite_note-nytimes20090906-197] The government's "Islamic Endowment Ministry" has deployed Virtue Committee members to warn citizens of the dangers of immodest dress, card playing, and dating.[^cite_note-bloomberg-217] There are no government laws imposing dress and other moral standards, and the Hamas education ministry reversed one effort to impose Islamic dress on students.[^cite_note-nytimes20090906-197] There has also been successful resistance to attempts by local Hamas officials to impose Islamic dress on women.[^cite_note-218] Hamas officials deny having any plans to impose Islamic law, one legislator stating that "What you are seeing are incidents, not policy," and that Islamic law is the desired standard "but we believe in persuasion".[^cite_note-bloomberg-217]&#x20;

In 2013, [UNRWA](32270.md) canceled its annual marathon in Gaza after Hamas prohibited women from participating in the race.[^cite_note-219][^cite_note-220]&#x20;

### In the West Bank

In 2005, the human rights organization [Freemuse](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Freemuse\&action=edit\&redlink=1) released a report titled "Palestine: [Taliban](30635.md)-like attempts to censor music", which said that [Palestinian musicians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Music_of_Palestine) feared that harsh religious laws against music and concerts will be imposed since Hamas group scored political gains in the Palestinian Authority local elections of 2005.[^cite_note-freemuse-221]&#x20;

The attempt by Hamas to dictate a cultural code of conduct in the 1980s and early 1990s led to a violent fighting between different Palestinian sectors. Hamas members reportedly burned down stores that stocked videos they deemed indecent and destroyed books they described as "heretical".[^cite_note-barel-222]&#x20;

In 2005, an outdoor music-and-dance performance in [Qalqiliya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qalqiliya) was suddenly banned by the Hamas-led municipality, for the reason that such an event would be "[haram](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haram)", i.e. forbidden by Islam.[^cite_note-otterbeck-223] The municipality also ordered that music no longer be played in the Qalqiliya zoo, and mufti Akrameh Sabri issued a [religious edict](11259.md) affirming the municipality decision.[^cite_note-barel-222] In response, the Palestinian national poet [Mahmoud Darwish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahmoud_Darwish) warned that "There are Taliban-type elements in our society, and this is a very dangerous sign."[^cite_note-freemuse-221][^cite_note-darwish-224][^cite_note-newhumanist-225]&#x20;

The Palestinian columnist Mohammed Abd Al-Hamid, a resident of Ramallah, wrote that this religious coercion could cause the migration of artists, and said "The religious fanatics in [Algeria](358.md) destroyed every cultural symbol, shattered statues and rare works of art and liquidated intellectuals and artists, reporters and authors, ballet dancers and singers—are we going to imitate the Algerian and Afghani examples?"[^cite_note-barel-222]&#x20;

### Erdoğan's Turkey as a role model

Some Hamas members have stated that the model of Islamic government that Hamas seeks to emulate is that of Turkey under the rule of [Recep Tayyip Erdoğan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Recep_Tayyip_Erdo%C4%9Fan). The foremost members to distance Hamas from the practices of the Taliban and to publicly support the Erdoğan model were [Ahmed Yousef](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ahmed_Yousef) and [Ghazi Hamad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ghazi_Hamad), advisers to Prime Minister Hanieh.[^cite_note-Sayigh-226][^cite_note-227] Yusuf, the Hamas deputy foreign minister, reflected this goal in an interview with a Turkish newspaper, stating that while foreign public opinion equates Hamas with the Taliban or [al-Qaeda](1921.md), the analogy is inaccurate. Yusuf described the Taliban as "opposed to everything", including education and women's rights, while Hamas wants to establish good relations between the religious and secular elements of society and strives for human rights, democracy and an open society.[^cite_note-228] According to professor [Yezid Sayigh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yezid_Sayigh) of [King's College](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/King%27s_College_London) in London, how influential this view is within Hamas is uncertain, since both Ahmad Yousef and Ghazi Hamad were dismissed from their posts as advisers to Hamas Prime Minister [Ismail Hanieh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ismail_Hanieh) in October 2007.[^cite_note-Sayigh-226] Both have since been appointed to other prominent positions within the Hamas government. Khaled al-Hroub of the West Bank-based and anti-Hamas[^cite_note-229] Palestinian daily *Al Ayyam* added that despite claims by Hamas leaders that it wants to repeat the Turkish model of Islam, "what is happening on the ground in reality is a replica of the Taliban model of Islam."[^cite_note-230][^cite_note-Reuters_Erdogan-231]&#x20;

## Charter and policy documents

### 1988 charter

Main article: [1988 Hamas charter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1988_Hamas_charter)

See also: [Calls for the destruction of Israel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Calls_for_the_destruction_of_Israel)

Hamas published its charter in August 1988, wherein it defined itself as a chapter of the Muslim Brotherhood and its desire to establish "an Islamic state throughout Palestine".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKabahā2014324-232] The foundational document was written by a single individual, an "old guard" member of the Muslim Brotherhood in Gaza isolated from the outside world, and it was made public without going through the usual prior consultation, revision or consensus process, which Hamas leaders regretted in later years.[^cite_note-233] It was then signed on 18 August 1988. It [compares](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Comparisons_between_Israel_and_Nazi_Germany) [Israeli attacks on civilians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israeli_war_crimes) to that by Nazi Germany.[^cite_note-234] The charter also claims all of historical Palestine[^cite_note-235][^cite_note-236][^cite_note-237][^cite_note-238][^cite_note-FOOTNOTELitvak1998153-239][^cite_note-Weimann-240] but promises religious coexistence under Islam's rule.[^cite_note-241][^cite_note-242] Article 6 states that the movement's aim is to "raise the banner of [Allah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allah) over every inch of Palestine, for under the wing of Islam followers of all religions can coexist in security and safety where their lives, possessions and rights are concerned".[^cite_note-Yale-152][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMishalSela2006178-243] The charter rejects a [two-state solution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Two-state_solution), stating that [the conflict](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israeli_Palestinian_conflict) cannot be resolved "except through [jihad](16203.md)".&#x20;

Many scholars have pointed out that both the 1988 Hamas's charter and the [Likud](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Likud) party platform sought full control of the land, thus denouncing the two-state solution.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBeinart2012219,_n.53-128][^cite_note-Emmett-129][^cite_note-Chomsky2010-130]&#x20;

### 2017 charter

Main article: [2017 Hamas charter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2017_Hamas_charter)

In May 2017, Hamas published a document titled "[A Document of General Principles and Policies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A_Document_of_General_Principles_and_Policies)". While this policy document was much shorter than the 1988 charter, and Hamas leaders stated that it did not replace the 1988 charter,[^cite_note-no-softened-179] it covers some of the same topics and is referred to as the "2017 charter".[^cite_note-Hroub-60] It accepted a Palestinian state in the 1967 borders, [without recognizing Israel](#Recognition_of_Israel), which is seen by many as being consistent with a [two-state solution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Two-state_solution),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELybarger2020199-244][^cite_note-Baconi-108-52] while others state that Hamas retains the long-term objective of establishing one state in former [Mandatory Palestine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mandatory_Palestine).[^cite_note-Alsoos2-53]<sup>: 837, 839 </sup> The charter also argued that armed resistance to occupation is supported by international law.[^cite_note-Hroub-60][^cite_note-245][^cite_note-Alsoos2-53]<sup>: 837, 839 </sup>[^cite_note-charter2017-246] Hamas has described these changes as adaptation within a specific context, as opposed to abandonment of its principles.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESeurat202218-247]&#x20;

While the [1988 Hamas charter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1988_Hamas_charter) was widely described as [antisemitic](1078.md),[^cite_note-Hamed2023-54] Hamas's 2017 charter removed the antisemitic language and said Hamas's struggle was with [Zionists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zionists), not Jews.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESeurat201917-56][^cite_note-Haaretz20170503-57][^cite_note-Spitka2023-58][^cite_note-AlJazeera20170506-59] Some sources maintain its condemnation of Zionists is antisemitic:[^cite_note-hoffman-248][^cite_note-Spitka2023-58] it describes Zionism as the enemy of all Muslims and a danger to international security, what author J.S. Spoerl in 2020 has disqualified as "hardly (...) a serious repudiation of anti-Semitism".[^cite_note-Spoerl_2020_pp._210–244-61] Hroub, though not responding directly, disagrees, writing that the 2017 document shows that Hamas is stressing the nationalist/resistance aspects of its purpose, providing a "clear assertion of the right to a national liberation struggle on the basis of international law."[^cite_note-Hroub-60] Similarly, scholar Joas Wagemakers argues that the 2017 charter clearly omits all antisemitic elements and instead takes a nationalist viewpoint.[^cite_note-249] Scholar Leïla Seurat and analyst Tareq Baconi also stress that the charter broadens the movement's tactics by restricting the primacy of armed struggle and complementing it with peaceful forms of resistance.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESeurat201917-56][^cite_note-250]&#x20;

## Organization

### Leadership and structure

Main article: [List of leaders of Hamas](77528143.md)



Map of key Hamas leadership nodes. 2010.

Hamas inherited from its predecessor a tripartite structure under a [Shura Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shura_Council): social services; religious training; military operations. Traditionally it had four distinct functions: (a) a charitable social welfare division (*dawah*); (b) a military division for procuring weapons and undertaking operations (*al-Mujahideen al Filastinun*); (c) a security service (*Jehaz Aman*); and (d) a media branch (*A'alam*).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELevitt200610–11-251] Hamas has both an internal leadership within the West Bank and the Gaza Strip, and an external leadership, split between a Gaza group directed by [Mousa Abu Marzook](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mousa_Abu_Marzook) from his exile first in Damascus and then in Egypt, and a Kuwaiti group (*Kuwaidia*) under [Khaled Mashal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khaled_Mashal).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELevitt200611–12-252] The Kuwaiti group of Palestinian exiles began to receive extensive funding from the [Gulf States](3088555.md) after its leader Mashal broke with [Yasser Arafat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yasser_Arafat)'s decision to side with [Saddam Hussein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saddam_Hussein) in the [Invasion of Kuwait](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Invasion_of_Kuwait), with Mashal insisting that Iraq withdraw.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERoy201330-253] On 6 May 2017, [Hamas' Shura Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Majlis_al-Shura) chose [Ismail Haniyeh](41207463.md) to become the new leader, to replace Mashal.[^cite_note-254]&#x20;

The exact structure of the organization is unclear as it is shrouded in a veil of secrecy in order to conceal operational activities. Formally, Hamas maintains the wings are separate and independent, but this has been questioned. It has been argued that its wings are both separate and combined for reasons of internal and external political necessity. Communication between the political and military wings of Hamas is made difficult by the thoroughness of Israeli intelligence surveillance and the existence of an extensive base of informants. After the assassination of [Abdel Aziz al-Rantisi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abdel_Aziz_al-Rantisi) the political direction of the militant wing was diminished and field commanders were given wider discretion over operations.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDavis201644–45-255]&#x20;

#### Shura Council and Political Bureau

Hamas's overarching governing body is the [Majlis al-Shura](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Majlis_al-Shura) (Shura Council), based on the [Quranic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quran) concept of consultation and popular assembly (*[shura](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shura)*), which Hamas leaders argue provides for democracy within an Islamic framework.[^cite_note-256] As the organization grew more complex and Israeli pressure increased, the Shura Council was renamed the General Consultative Council, with members elected from local council groups. The council elects the 15-member Political Bureau (*al-Maktab al-Siyasi*)[^cite_note-Mandaville-257] that makes decisions for Hamas. Representatives come from Gaza, the West Bank, leaders in exile and [Israeli prisons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestinian_prisoners_in_Israel#Administrative_detention).[^cite_note-Berti-258] The Political Bureau was based in [Damascus](8914.md) until January 2012, when Hamas's support for the [Syrian opposition to Bashar al-Assad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syrian_opposition_to_Bashar_al-Assad) during the [Syrian civil war](30741795.md) led to the office's relocation to [Qatar](8238258.md).[^cite_note-Berti-258][^cite_note-259]&#x20;

### Finances and funding

See also: [Blockade of the Gaza Strip](19224310.md), [Iranian support for Hamas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iranian_support_for_Hamas), [Israeli support for Hamas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israeli_support_for_Hamas), [Taxation in Palestine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taxation_in_Palestine), and [Fatah–Hamas reconciliation process](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fatah%E2%80%93Hamas_reconciliation_process)

As of 2023, half of Hamas funding came from transfers from the [Palestinian Authority](24093.md). Other sources include taxes in Gaza, transfers from Qatar, Iran and charities.[^cite_note-260] Hamas assumed the administration of Gaza's *[waqf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Waqf)* properties, endowments which extend over 10% of real estate in Gaza, with 2,000 acres of agricultural land held in religious trusts, together with shops, rentable apartments and public buildings.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAbu-Amr19938-261] As of 2023 Hamas has an investment portfolio worth US\$0.5-\$1 billion, including assets in Sudan, Turkey, Saudi Arabia, Algeria and the UAE.[^cite_note-262] Hamas has denied this.[^cite_note-263] In 2024, financial activity in Gaza was carried out via money changers to bypass international financial authorities.[^cite_note-264] The [European Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Council) added six people to its sanctions list for helping fund Hamas, including an [Iranian Revolutionary Guard Corps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iranian_Revolutionary_Guard_Corps) official. Three businesses were added, including companies in Spain and Sudan that functioned as fronts.[^cite_note-265][^cite_note-266]&#x20;

In the first five years of the 1st Intifada, the Gaza economy, 50% of which depended on external sources of income, plummeted by 40% as Israel closed its labor market, and remittances from the [Palestinian expatriates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestinian_diaspora) in Gulf countries dried up following the 1991–92 [Gulf War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gulf_War).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERoy199321-267] At the 1993 Philadelphia conference, Hamas leaders' statements indicated that they read [George H. W. Bush](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_H._W._Bush)'s [New World Order](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_world_order_\(politics\)) as embodying a [tacit aim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_World_Order_\(conspiracy_theory\)) to destroy Islam, and therefore funding should focus on enhancing the Islamic roots of Palestinian society and promoting jihad, which also means zeal for social justice, in the occupied territories.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELevitt2006148-268] Hamas became fastidious about maintaining separate resourcing for its military, political and social services branches.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVittori201172-269] It has a holding company in East Jerusalem (*Beit al-Mal*), a 20% stake in Al Aqsa International Bank which served as its financial arm, the Sunuqrut Global Group and the al-Ajouli money-changing firm.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVittori201173-270]&#x20;

By 2011, Hamas's budget, US\$70 million, derived 85% from foreign, rather than Palestinian sources.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVittori201173-270] Only two Israeli-Palestinian sources figure in a list seized in 2004, while other contributors were donor bodies located in Jordan, Qatar, Kuwait, Saudi Arabia, Britain, Germany, the US, UAE, Italy and France. Much of the money comes from sources that direct their assistance to what Hamas describes as its charitable work for Palestinians, but investments in support of its ideological position are also relevant, with Gulf States and Saudi Arabia prominent in the latter. Hamas taps money from corporations, criminal organizations and financial networks that support terror.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELevitt2006143–44-271] It is alleged it engages in cigarette and drug smuggling, multimedia copyright infringement and credit card fraud.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVittori201173-270] The US, Israel and the EU have shut down charities and organs that channel money to Hamas, such as the [Holy Land Foundation for Relief and Development](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holy_Land_Foundation_for_Relief_and_Development).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEClarke201597-272][^cite_note-273]&#x20;

About half of Hamas's funding came from Persian Gulf states up to the mid-2000s. Saudi Arabia supplied half the Hamas budget in the early 2000s,[^cite_note-Burfeindt-274] but, under US pressure, cut its funding by cracking down on Islamic charities and private donor transfers in 2004,[^cite_note-Vittori-275] which by 2006 drastically reduced the flow. Iran and Syria, in the aftermath of Hamas's 2006 electoral victory, stepped in to fill the shortfall.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELevitt2006173-276][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGleisBerti2012156-277] Saudi funding remained supportive of Hamas as a Sunni group but chose to provide more assistance to the PNA, the electoral loser, when the EU responded to the outcome by suspending its aid.[^cite_note-278] During the 1980s, Iran provided 10% of Hamas's funding.[^cite_note-Burfeindt-274] It accounted for \$22 million, over a quarter of Hamas's budget, by the late 2000s.[^cite_note-Vittori-275] Iran preferred direct financing to operative groups rather than charities, requiring video proof of attacks.[^cite_note-Vittori-275][^cite_note-FOOTNOTELevitt2006172–74-279] Much of Iran's funding is channeled through [Hezbollah](13919.md).[^cite_note-Vittori-275] After 2006, Iran's willingness to fill the gap created by the drying up of Saudi funding also reflected the geopolitical tensions between the two, since, though Shiite, Iran was supporting a Sunni group closely linked with the Saudi kingdom.[^cite_note-280] The US imposed sanctions on Iran's Bank Saderat, alleging it had funneled hundreds of millions to Hamas.[^cite_note-281] The US has expressed concerns that Hamas obtains funds through Palestinian and Lebanese sympathizers of Arab descent in the [Foz do Iguaçu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foz_do_Igua%C3%A7u) area of Latin America, an area associated with arms trading, drug trafficking, contraband, the manufacture of counterfeit goods, money-laundering and currency fraud. Confirmatory information of Hamas operational presence there is lacking.[^cite_note-282]&#x20;

After 2009, [sanctions on Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sanctions_on_Iran) made funding difficult, forcing Hamas to rely on religious donations in the West Bank, Qatar, and Saudi Arabia. Tens of millions of dollars raised in the Gulf states were transferred through the [Rafah Border Crossing](10475618.md). These were insufficient to cover the cost of governing the Strip and running the al-Qassam Brigades, and when tensions arose with Iran over support of Assad in Syria, Iran dropped its financial assistance to the government, restricting its funding to the military wing, which meant a drop from \$150 million in 2012 to \$60 million the following year. A further drop occurred in 2015 when Hamas criticised Iran's role in the [Yemeni Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yemeni_Civil_War_\(2015%E2%80%93present\)).[^cite_note-283]&#x20;

In 2017, the PA government imposed sanctions against Gaza, including cutting off salaries to PA employees. The PA said it would not pay for the electricity and fuel that Israel supplies to the Gaza Strip, but backtracked.[^cite_note-284] The Israeli government has allowed millions of dollars from Qatar to be funneled through Israel to Hamas, to replace the millions of dollars the PA had stopped transferring to Hamas. Israeli Prime Minister [Benjamin Netanyahu](56472.md) explained that letting it go through Israel meant it could not be used for terrorism: "Now that we are supervising, we know it's going to humanitarian causes."[^cite_note-285]&#x20;

### Social services wing

Hamas developed its social welfare programme by replicating the model established by Egypt's Muslim Brotherhood. For Hamas, charity and the development of one's community are both prescribed by religion and to be understood as forms of resistance.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDunning2016136-286] In Islamic tradition, *dawah* (lit. transl.
