"the call to God") obliges the faithful to reach out to others by both proselytising and by charitable works, and typically the latter center on the mosques which make use of both *[waqf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Waqf)* endowment resources and charitable donations to fund grassroots services such as nurseries, schools, orphanages, soup kitchens, women's activities, library services and even sporting clubs within a larger context of preaching and political discussions.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELevitt200616–23-287] In the 1990s, some 85% of its budget was allocated to the provision of social services.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPhillips201178-288] Hamas has been called perhaps the most significant social services actor in Palestine. By 2000, Hamas or its affiliated charities ran roughly 40% of the social institutions in the West Bank and Gaza and, with other Islamic charities, by 2005, was supporting 120,000 individuals with monthly financial support in Gaza.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShitrit201571-289] Part of the appeal of these institutions is that they fill a vacuum in the administration by the PLO of the Palestinian territories, which had failed to cater to the demand for jobs and broad social services, and is widely viewed as corrupt.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPhillips201175-290] As late as 2005, the budget of Hamas, drawing on global charity contributions, was mostly tied up in covering running expenses for its social programmes, which extended from the supply of housing, food and water for the needy to more general functions such as financial aid, medical assistance, educational development and religious instruction. A certain accounting flexibility allowed these funds to cover both charitable causes and military operations, permitting transfer from one to the other.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDavis201647ff-291]&#x20;

The *dawah* infrastructure itself was understood, within the Palestinian context, as providing the soil from which a militant opposition to the occupation would flower.[^cite_note-293] In this regard it differs from the rival [Palestinian Islamic Jihad](13093253.md) which focuses less on its social welfare network, and relies on spectacular attacks to recruit adherents.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELevitt200625–26-294] In 2007, through funding from Iran, Hamas managed to allocate at a cost of \$60 million, monthly stipends of \$100 for 100,000 workers, and a similar sum for 3,000 fishermen [laid idle by Israel's blockade](19224310.md#Effect_on_the_fishing_industry), plus grants totalling \$45 million to detainees and their families.[^cite_note-295] [Matthew Levitt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Matthew_Levitt) argues that Hamas grants to people are subject to a rigorous cost-benefit analysis of how beneficiaries will support Hamas, with those linked to militant activities receiving more than others.[^cite_note-296] Israel holds the families of suicide bombers accountable and bulldozes their homes, whereas the families of Hamas activists who have been killed or wounded during militant operations are given an initial, one-time grant varying between \$500–\$5,000, together with a \$100 monthly allowance. Rent assistance is also given to families whose homes have been destroyed by Israeli bombing though families unaffiliated with Hamas are said to receive less.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPhillips201181-297][^cite_note-FOOTNOTELevitt2006122–23-298]&#x20;

Until 2007, these activities extended to the West Bank, but, after a PLO crackdown, now continue exclusively in the Gaza Strip.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDavis201648-299] After the [2013 Egyptian coup d'état](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2013_Egyptian_coup_d%27%C3%A9tat) deposed the elected Muslim Brotherhood government of [Mohamed Morsi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohamed_Morsi) in 2013, Hamas found itself in a financial straitjacket and has since endeavoured to throw the burden of responsibility for public works infrastructure in the Gaza Strip back onto the Palestinian National Authority, but without success.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDavis201648–49-300]&#x20;

### Media

See also: [Media coverage of the Israeli–Palestinian conflict](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Media_coverage_of_the_Israeli%E2%80%93Palestinian_conflict), [Public diplomacy of Israel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_diplomacy_of_Israel), and [Palestinian propaganda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestinian_propaganda)

#### Al-Aqsa TV

Main article: [Al-Aqsa TV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Aqsa_TV)

See also: [Shehab News Agency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shehab_News_Agency) and [Tomorrow's Pioneers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tomorrow%27s_Pioneers)

[Al-Aqsa TV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Aqsa_TV) is a television channel founded by Hamas.[^cite_note-301] The station began broadcasting in the Gaza Strip on 9 January 2006,[^cite_note-news24_alaqsa_tv-302][^cite_note-adl_al_aqsa_tv-303] less than three weeks before the [Palestinian legislative elections](38945340.md). It has shown television programs, including some children's television, which deliver antisemitic messages.[^cite_note-304] Hamas has stated that the television station is "an independent media institution that often does not express the views of the Palestinian government headed by Ismail Haniyeh or of the Hamas movement", and that Hamas does not hold antisemitic views.[^cite_note-Hamas_Condemns_the_Holocaust-305] The programming includes ideologically tinged children's shows, news talk, and religiously inspired entertainment.[^cite_note-npr_al_aqsa_tv-306] According to the [Anti-Defamation League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-Defamation_League), the station promotes terrorist activity and incites hatred of Jews and Israelis.[^cite_note-adl_al_aqsa_tv-303] Al-Aqsa TV is headed by the controversial [Fathi Ahmad Hamad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fathi_Ahmad_Hamad), chairman of al-Ribat Communications and Artistic Productions—a Hamas-run company that also produces Hamas's radio station, *Voice of al-Aqsa*, and its biweekly newspaper, *The Message*.[^cite_note-guardian_johnson-307] Hamad has made a number of controversial comments, including a speech in which he reportedly stated: "you have Jews everywhere and we must attack every Jew on the globe by way of slaughter and killing."[^cite_note-308]&#x20;

#### Al-Fateh magazine

Main article: [Al-Fateh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Fateh)

*Al-Fateh* ("the conqueror") was a Hamas children's magazine, published biweekly in [London](17867.md), and posted online.[^cite_note-309][^cite_note-310] It began publication in September 2002, and its 108th issue was released in September 2007. The magazine featured stories, poems, riddles, and puzzles, and stated it is for "the young builders of the future".[^cite_note-OnlineTerrorists-311]&#x20;

The magazine had been accused of antisemitism, with the magazine repeatedly calling Jews things such as: "the most despicable of God’s creatures", "the lowest of the human race", "like a [cancer](105219.md) which has to be gotten rid of". The magazine also holds an anti-western and [revanchist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Revanchism) position, claiming that because of its morally justified cause the [muslim world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_world) will triumph over the [western world](21208200.md) and that the western world will collapse.[^cite_note-312]&#x20;

#### Social media

Hamas has traditionally presented itself as a voice of suffering of the Palestinian people. According to [*Time*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Time_\(magazine\)) magazine, a new social media strategy was employed in the wake of the [October 7 attacks](74998317.md): Hamas asserted itself as the dominant militant force in the Middle East by recording and [broadcasting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Broadcasting) the brutality of their attacks.[^cite_note-313]&#x20;

In June 2015, Hamas launched an English-language version of its official website.[^cite_note-314] Hamas' official website at hamas.ps was active as of September 2023, though it has since been taken offline.[^cite_note-\:7-315] In November 2023, Israeli social media accounts shared hamas.com, a website with videos and pictures of the October 7 attacks released by the [IDF](39237.md) that was claimed to be created by Hamas.[^cite_note-316] Fact-checking website [Misbar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Misbar) reported that Hamas had confirmed on [Telegram](41501305.md) that its official website is hamas.ps.[^cite_note-317][^cite_note-\:7-315]&#x20;

According to Dr. Harel Horev, historian and researcher of Palestinian affairs at [Tel Aviv University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tel_Aviv_University), Hamas has used social media to dehumanize Israelis/Jews. According to his research, Hamas took over the most popular accounts on Palestinian networks in a covert manner that did not reveal its involvement. This control gave it the ability to significantly influence the Palestinian discourse online through content that denies the humanity and right to life of Israelis. These included posters, songs and videos glorifying threats; computer games that encourage the murder of Jews; training videos for carrying out effective and indiscriminate stabbing and shooting attacks; and anti-Semitic cartoons as a central means of dehumanizing the Israeli/Jew in the Palestinian online discourse.[^cite_note-318][^cite_note-319]&#x20;

Following pressure from pro-Israel advocacy groups and tech giants Apple and Google, Telegram has blocked several Hamas-linked channels that violated app store policies regarding violent content.[^cite_note-320]&#x20;

### Internal security

See also: [Ministry of Interior (State of Palestine)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Interior_\(State_of_Palestine\))

The General Security Service, formally part of the Hamas political party, operates akin to a governmental body within Gaza. Under the direct oversight of Hamas leader Yahya Sinwar, it conducts extensive surveillance on Palestinians, compiling files on various individuals including journalists and government critics. This secret police force relies on a network of informants and employs tactics such as censorship and surveillance to maintain control. Before the conflict with Israel, the unit reportedly had a monthly budget of \$120,000 and consisted of 856 personnel, including more than 160 individuals paid to spread Hamas propaganda and conduct online attacks against opponents.[^cite_note-NYTimes20240513-321]&#x20;

Other powerful internal security bodies in Gaza include Military Intelligence, which focuses on Israel, and the Internal Security Service, an arm of the [Interior Ministry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Interior_\(State_of_Palestine\)).[^cite_note-NYTimes20240513-321]&#x20;

During the [COVID-19 pandemic in Palestine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic_in_Palestine) Hamas implemented restrictions in a "[flattening the curve](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flattening_the_curve)" approach.[^cite_note-sama-322] The restrictions imposed were similar to most western countries, and more relaxed than the [zero COVID](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zero_COVID) policy implemented by [China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic_in_China) and [their neighbors](33583.md#Regional_offices).[^cite_note-Lockdown_option-323]&#x20;

## Qassam Brigades (military wing)

Main article: [Izz ad-Din al-Qassam Brigades](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Izz_ad-Din_al-Qassam_Brigades)



Weapons found in a mosque during [Operation Cast Lead](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Cast_Lead), according to the IDF.&#x20;

The [Izz ad-Din al-Qassam Brigades](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Izz_ad-Din_al-Qassam_Brigades) is Hamas' military wing.[^cite_note-Qassam_logo-324][^cite_note-325] While the number of members is known only to the Brigades leadership, Israel estimates the Brigades have a core of several hundred members who receive [military style training](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Military_training), including training in Iran and in [Syria](7515849.md) (before the Syrian Civil War).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGuidère2012173-326] Additionally, the brigades have an estimated 10,000–17,000 operatives,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShitrit201571-289][^cite_note-FOOTNOTENajibFriedrich2007106-327] other sources say 15,000–40,000 militants,[^cite_note-328][^cite_note-329] forming a backup force whenever circumstances call for reinforcements for the Brigade. Recruitment training lasts for two years.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGuidère2012173-326] The group's ideology outlines its aim as the liberation of Palestine and the restoration of Palestinian rights under the dispensations set forth in the Qur'an, and this translates into three policy priorities:&#x20;

> To evoke the spirit of Jihad (Resistance) among Palestinians, Arabs, and Muslims; to defend Palestinians and their land against the Zionist occupation and its manifestations; to liberate Palestinians and their land that was usurped by the Zionist occupation forces and settlers.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENajibFriedrich2007105-330]

According to its official stipulations, the Izz ad-Din al-Qassam Brigades' military operations are to be restricted to operating only inside Palestine, engaging with Israeli soldiers,[^cite_note-331] and in exercising the right of self-defense against armed settlers. They are to avoid civilian targets, to respect the enemy's humanity by refraining from mutilation, defacement or excessive killing, and to avoid targeting Westerners either in the occupied zones or beyond.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENajibFriedrich2007105–06-332]&#x20;



Exercise of al-Qassam Brigades in Gaza City, 27 January 2013

Down to 2007, the Brigades are estimated to have lost some 800 operatives in conflicts with Israeli forces. The leadership has been consistently undermined by targeted assassinations. Aside from [Yahya Ayyash](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yahya_Ayyash) (5 January 1996), it has lost [Emad Akel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emad_Akel) (24 November 1993), [Salah Shehade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salah_Shehade) (23 July 2002), [Ibrahim al-Makadmeh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ibrahim_al-Makadmeh) (8 March 2003), [Ismail Abu Shanab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ismail_Abu_Shanab) (21 August 2003), [Ahmed Yassin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ahmed_Yassin) (22 March 2004), and [Abdel Aziz al-Rantisi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abdel_Aziz_al-Rantisi) (17 April 2004).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENajibFriedrich2007107-333][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHuestonPierpaoliZahar201467-334]&#x20;

The Izz ad-Din al-Qassam Brigades groups its fighters in 4–5 man cells, which in turn are integrated into companies and battalions. Unlike the political section, which is split between an internal and external structure, the Brigades are under a local Palestinian leadership, and disobedience with the decisions taken by the political leadership have been relatively rare.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENajibFriedrich2007107–08-335]&#x20;

Although the Izz al-Din al-Qassam Brigades are an integral part of Hamas, the exact nature of the relationship is hotly debated.[^cite_note-Mandaville-257][^cite_note-FOOTNOTELevitt200889ff.-336] They appear to operate at times independently of Hamas, exercising a certain autonomy.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDavis2004100-337][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHerrick2011?-338][^cite_note-339] Some cells have independent links with the external leadership, enabling them to bypass the hierarchical command chain and political leadership in Gaza.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGunning2007123–55-340] Ilana Kass and Bard O'Neill, likening Hamas's relationship with the Brigades to the political party [Sinn Féin](28175.md)'s relationship to the military arm of the [Irish Republican Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irish_Republican_Army), quote a senior Hamas official as stating: "The Izz al-Din al-Qassam Brigade is a separate armed military wing, which has its own leaders who do not take their orders from Hamas and do not tell us of their plans in advance."[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKassO'Neill1997267-341][^cite_note-344]&#x20;

### Gaza forces, October 2023

During the Gaza war, the IDF published its intelligence about the Hamas military in the Strip.[^cite_note-ToI23-345] They put the strength of the Qassam Brigades there at the start of the war at 30,000 fighters, organized by area in five brigades, consisting in total of 24 battalions and c. 140 companies.[^cite_note-ToI23-345] Each regional brigade had a number of strongholds and outposts, and included specialised arrays for rocket firing, anti-tank missiles, air defenses, snipers, and engineering.[^cite_note-ToI23-345]&#x20;

## Symbols



Hamas flags at the 25th anniversary rally in 2012

The flag of Hamas is a green field ([a traditionally respected color in Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Green_in_Islam)) charged in the middle with the writing of the *[Shahada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shahada)*, an Islamic statement of faith, in white calligraphic script: "There is no god but [God](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/God_in_Islam)" and "[Muhammad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad) is the messenger of God".[^cite_note-346][^cite_note-347]&#x20;

The emblems of their political[^cite_note-wwr8z0-348][^cite_note-npr20070619-349] and military wings are distinct from each other.[^cite_note-militant_logo-350][^cite_note-toi_logo-351][^cite_note-קסאם-352] The emblem of Hamas' political wing features Islamic and militaristic motifs. It shows two crossed swords in front of the [central building](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dome_of_the_rock) of the [Al-Aqsa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Aqsa) mosque complex, in Jerusalem. The mosque is framed by two Palestinian flags that feature the two statements that comprise the *Shahada*.[^cite_note-npr20070619-349][^cite_note-wwr8z0-348] Above Al Aqsa is a map of Palestine, matching the borders of [Mandatory Palestine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mandatory_Palestine). Immediately below the Dome it reads "Palestine" and below that in the green banner: "Islamic Resistance Movement – Hamas".[^cite_note-353] The emblem of their [Al-Qassam Brigades](1703333.md) militant wing does not include a map or a Palestinian flag, the militant wing emblem is a cartoon drawing of a man holding an [M16 rifle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M16_rifle) and a [Quran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quran), with his face mostly covered by a black and white [Palestinian keffiyeh](47446641.md).[^cite_note-Qassam_logo-324][^cite_note-toi_logo-351][^cite_note-קסאם-352] He is standing in front of a green flag and the [golden dome](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dome_of_the_rock) of the [Al-Aqsa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Aqsa) mosque, but the building is more stylised than it is in the political wing emblem.[^cite_note-militant_logo-350][^cite_note-toi_logo-351][^cite_note-קסאם-352]&#x20;

## Violence

Hamas has used both political activities and violence in pursuit of its goals. For example, while politically engaged in the 2006 Palestinian Territories parliamentary election campaign, Hamas stated in its election manifesto that it was prepared to use "armed resistance to end [the occupation](2781576.md)".[^cite_note-SMF22-354] Hamas has repeatedly justified its violence by arguing "People under occupation have a right to resist that occupation".[^cite_note-sen-355] Hamas also argues its armed resistance only started after decades of Israeli occupation.[^cite_note-sen-355]&#x20;

From 2000 to 2004, Hamas was responsible for killing nearly 400 Israelis and wounding more than 2,000 in 425 attacks, according to the Israeli Ministry of Foreign Affairs. From 2001 through May 2008, Hamas launched more than 3,000 Qassam rockets and 2,500 mortar attacks into Israel.[^cite_note-fox1-356]&#x20;

### Attacks on civilians



Aftermath of 1996 [Jaffa Road bus bombings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jaffa_Road_bus_bombings) in which 26 people were killed

Hamas have committed massacres targeting Israeli civilians. Hamas's most deadly suicide bombing was the [Passover Massacre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Passover_massacre), which occurred in a [Netanya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Netanya) hotel on 27 March 2002, at a [Passover Seder](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Passover_Seder) in which 30 people were killed and 140 were wounded.&#x20;

Hamas has defended suicide attacks as a legitimate aspect of its [asymmetric warfare](46863.md) against Israel. In 2003, according to [Stephen Atkins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stephen_Atkins), Hamas resumed suicide bombings in Israel as a retaliatory measure after the failure of peace talks and an Israeli campaign targeting members of the upper echelon of the Hamas leadership.[^cite_note-358] In a 2002 report, Human Rights Watch stated that Hamas leaders "should be held accountable" for "war crimes and crimes against humanity" committed by the al-Qassam Brigades.[^cite_note-hrw\.org-bombing-359][^cite_note-360][^cite_note-361]&#x20;

In 2008, Hamas leader [Khaled Mashal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khaled_Mashal), offered that Hamas would attack only military targets if the IDF would stop causing the deaths of Palestinian civilians.[^cite_note-362] Following a 19 June 2008, ceasefire, the al-Qassam Brigades ended its rocket attacks and arrested Fatah militants in Gaza who had continued sporadic rocket and mortar attacks against Israel. The al-Qassam Brigades resumed the attacks after the 4 November Israeli incursion into Gaza.[^cite_note-ITIC-84][^cite_note-ynetnews.com-85]&#x20;



The 2023 [Re'im music festival massacre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Re%27im_music_festival_massacre) left 364 people dead with many others wounded or taken hostage

During the [October 7 attacks](74998317.md), Hamas infiltrated homes, shot civilians en masse, and took scores of Israeli civilians and soldiers as hostages into Gaza.[^cite_note-363][^cite_note-364] According to [Human Rights Watch](46530.md), the deliberate targeting of civilians, indiscriminate attacks, and taking of civilians as hostages amount to [war crimes](48626.md) under international humanitarian law.[^cite_note-365] During its October 2023 offensive against Israel, Hamas massacred 364 people at the [Re'im music festival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Re%27im_music_festival_massacre), while [abducting](75025451.md) others.[^cite_note-366][^cite_note-Morris2023-367] During the same offensive, it also was reported that Hamas had massacred the population of the [Kfar Aza](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kfar_Aza) kibbutz.[^cite_note-368] About 10 percent of the residents of the [Be'eri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Be%27eri) kibbutz were killed.[^cite_note-369] Hamas militants attacked the [Psyduck festival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Psyduck_music_festival_massacre), that took place near kibutz Nir Oz, killing 17 [Israeli](181296.md) partygoers.[^cite_note-370] Video footage shows children being deliberately killed during the kibbutz attacks,[^cite_note-371] as well as what appears to be an attempt to decapitate a living person using a garden hoe.[^cite_note-372] Forensic teams who examined bodies of victims said many bodies showed signs of torture as well as [sexual and gender-based violence](75436869.md), and testimonies to this effect were also collected by Israeli police.[^cite_note-373][^cite_note-374][^cite_note-375][^cite_note-376] Haaretz later reported that forensic pathologists who examined bodies of the victims taken the Shura Base for identification found "no signs on any of those bodies attesting to sexual relations having taken place or of mutilation of genitalia." Not all bodies could be fully examined given the lack of forensic pathologists, and for some conclusions could not be drawn given their deteriorated state.[^cite_note-377] Israeli forensic pathologists in charge of the process clarified later that all bodies had been examined, and some were disfigured or burned.[^cite_note-378] Some of the testimonies were later proven to be false.[^cite_note-379][^cite_note-beri-false-380][^cite_note-381] Detailed reports by the UN and *[The Times](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Times)* concluded that Israel's claims about the scale and formally sanctioned, systematic nature of sexual assaults did not stand up to scrutiny.[^cite_note-382][^cite_note-OHCHR3-383][^cite_note-Weiniger-384]&#x20;

According to a 2010 [Human Rights Watch](46530.md) report, 15 civilians had been killed in rocket attacks by Hamas and other groups since 2001. Hamas has claimed that its rockets are only aimed at military targets, and that the low quality of its weapons was the cause of the inadvertent civilian casualties. According to [Human Rights Watch](46530.md), statements by Hamas leaders shed doubt on these claims and indicate that targeting of civilians was not accidental. From January 2009, following [Operation Cast Lead](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Cast_Lead), Hamas largely stopped launching rocket attacks on Israel and has on at least two occasions arrested members of other groups who have launched rockets, "showing that it has the ability to impose the law when it wants".[^cite_note-385] In February 2010, Hamas issued a statement regretting any harm that may have befallen Israeli civilians as a result of Palestinian rocket attacks during the Gaza war. It maintained that its rocket attacks had been aimed at Israeli military targets but lacked accuracy and hence sometimes hit civilian areas. Israel responded that Hamas had boasted repeatedly of targeting and murdering civilians in the media.[^cite_note-386]&#x20;

According to one report, commenting on the 2014 conflict, "nearly all the 2,500–3,000 rockets and mortars Hamas has fired at Israel since the start of the war seem to have been aimed at towns", including an attack on "a kibbutz collective farm close to the Gaza border", in which an Israeli child was killed.[^cite_note-Training_Manual-387] Former Israeli Lt. Col. Jonathan D. Halevi stated that "Hamas has expressed pride in aiming long-range rockets at strategic targets in Israel including the nuclear reactor in Dimona, the chemical plants in Haifa, and Ben-Gurion Airport", which "could have caused thousands" of Israeli casualties "if successful".[^cite_note-388]&#x20;

In July 2008, [Barack Obama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barack_Obama), then the Democratic presidential candidate, said: "If somebody was sending rockets into my house, where my two daughters sleep at night, I'm going to do everything in my power to stop that, and I would expect Israelis to do the same thing."[^cite_note-389] On 28 December 2008, Secretary of State [Condoleezza Rice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Condoleezza_Rice) said in a statement: "the United States strongly condemns the repeated rocket and mortar attacks against Israel."[^cite_note-390] On 2 March 2009, Secretary of State [Hillary Clinton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hillary_Clinton) condemned the attacks.[^cite_note-391]&#x20;

On 7 October 2023, Hamas claimed responsibility for a barrage of missile attacks originating from the Gaza Strip.[^cite_note-392]&#x20;

### Guerrilla warfare



Hamas anti-tank rockets, captured by Israel Defense Forces during Operation Protective Edge

Hamas has made great use of [guerrilla tactics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guerrilla_tactics) in the Gaza Strip and to a lesser degree the West Bank.[^cite_note-ynetnews_weight-393] It has successfully adapted these techniques over the years since its inception. According to a 2006 report by rival Fatah party, Hamas had smuggled between several hundred and 1,300 tons of advanced rockets, along with other weaponry, into Gaza.[^cite_note-ynetnews_weight-393]&#x20;

Hamas has used [IEDs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/IEDs) and [anti-tank rockets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-tank_rocket) against the [IDF](39237.md) in Gaza. The latter include standard [RPG-7](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/RPG-7) warheads and home-made rockets such as the [Al-Bana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Bana), Al-[Batar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Batar) and Al-[Yasin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yasin_\(RPG\)). The IDF has a difficult, if not impossible, time trying to find hidden weapons caches in Palestinian areas—this is due to the high local support base Hamas enjoys.[^cite_note-394]&#x20;

### Extrajudicial killings of rivals

See also: [Capital punishment in the Gaza Strip](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Capital_punishment_in_the_Gaza_Strip)

In addition to killing Israeli civilians and armed forces, Hamas has also murdered suspected Palestinian Israel collaborators and Fatah rivals.[^cite_note-395][^cite_note-396] According to the [Associated Press](18935732.md), collaborating with Israel is a crime punishable by death in Gaza.[^cite_note-397] Hundreds of Palestinians were executed by both Hamas and Fatah during the First Intifada.[^cite_note-398] In the wake of the 2006 Israeli conflict with Gaza, Hamas was accused of systematically rounding up, torturing and summarily executing Fatah supporters suspected of supplying information to Israel. Human Rights Watch estimates several hundred Gazans were "maimed" and tortured in the aftermath of the conflict. Seventy-three Gazan men accused of "collaborating" had their arms and legs broken by "unidentified perpetrators", and 18 Palestinians accused of helping Israel were executed by Hamas security officials in the first days of the conflict.[^cite_note-HRW1-399][^cite_note-btselem.org-400][^cite_note-401] In November 2012, Hamas's Izzedine al-Qassam brigade publicly executed six Gaza residents accused of collaborating with Israel. According to the witnesses, six alleged informers were shot dead one by one in [Gaza City](55387.md), while the corpse of the sixth victim was tied by a cable to the back of a motorcycle and dragged through the streets.[^cite_note-402] In 2013, Human Rights Watch issued a statement condemning Hamas for not investigating and giving a proper trial to the six men. Their statement was released the day before Hamas issued a deadline for "collaborators" to turn themselves in, or they will be pursued "without mercy".[^cite_note-403] During the [2014 Israel-Gaza conflict](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014_Israel-Gaza_conflict), Hamas executed at least 23 accused collaborators after three of its commanders were assassinated by Israeli forces, with [Amnesty International](18947898.md) also reporting instances of torture used by Hamas forces.[^cite_note-404][^cite_note-Executions-405] An Israeli source denied that any of the commanders had been targeted on the basis of human intelligence.[^cite_note-Not_collaborators-406]&#x20;

Killings of unarmed people have also occurred during Hamas-Fatah clashes.[^cite_note-407][^cite_note-408] NGOs have cited a number of [summary executions](1227309.md) as particular examples of violations of the rules of warfare, including the case of Muhammad Swairki, 28, a cook for Palestinian Authority Chairman Mahmoud Abbas's presidential guard, who was thrown to his death, with his hands and legs tied, from a 15-story apartment building in Gaza City.[^cite_note-409] Hamas security forces reportedly shoot and torture Palestinians who opposed Hamas rule in Gaza.[^cite_note-410] In one case, a Palestinian had criticized Hamas in a conversation on the street with some friends. Later that day, more than a dozen armed men with black masks and red [kaffiyeh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kaffiyeh) took the man from his home, and brought him to a solitary area where they shot him three times in the lower legs and ankles. The man told [Human Rights Watch](46530.md) that he was not politically active.[^cite_note-HRW1-399]&#x20;

On 14 August 2009, Hamas fighters [stormed the Mosque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Rafah_\(2009\)) of [extremist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamist_anti-Hamas_groups_in_the_Gaza_Strip) cleric [Abdel-Latif Moussa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abdel-Latif_Moussa).[^cite_note-411] The cleric was protected by at least 100 fighters from [Jund Ansar Allah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jund_Ansar_Allah) ("Army of the Helpers of God"), an [Islamist group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamist_anti-Hamas_groups_in_the_Gaza_Strip) with links to [Al-Qaeda](1921.md). [The resulting battle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Rafah_\(2009\)) left at least 13 people dead, including Moussa and six Hamas fighters, and 120 people injured.[^cite_note-412]&#x20;

According to [Palestinian president](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestinian_president) [Mahmoud Abbas](232595.md), during [2014 Israel–Gaza conflict](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014_Israel%E2%80%93Gaza_conflict), Hamas killed more than 120 Palestinian youths for defying house arrest imposed on them by Hamas, in addition to 30–40 Palestinians killed by Hamas in extrajudicial executions after accusing them of being collaborators with Israel.[^cite_note-413] Referring to the killing of suspected collaborators, a Shin Bet official stated that "not even one" of those executed by Hamas provided any intelligence to Israel, while the Shin Bet officially "confirmed that those executed during Operation Protective Edge had all been held in prison in Gaza in the course of the hostilities".[^cite_note-Not_collaborators-406]&#x20;

## Criticism

Main article: [Criticism of Hamas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Criticism_of_Hamas)

See also: [Use of human shields by Hamas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Use_of_human_shields_by_Hamas), [Allegations of genocide in the October 7 attacks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allegations_of_genocide_in_the_October_7_attacks), and [Hamas baby beheading hoax](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hamas_baby_beheading_hoax)

Aside from its use of [political violence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_violence) in pursuit of its goals, Hamas has been [widely criticised for a variety of reasons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Criticism_of_Hamas), including the use of [antisemitic](1078.md) [hate speech](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hate_speech) by its representatives; its frequent [calls for the destruction of Israel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Calls_for_the_destruction_of_Israel) by force; its reported [use of human shields](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Use_of_human_shields_by_Hamas)[^cite_note-414] and [child combatants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Child_combatants) as part of its military operations;[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELevitt2006124–134-415] and its restriction of [political freedoms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_freedom) within the Gaza Strip and [human rights abuses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_rights_abuses).[^cite_note-416]&#x20;

After the start of the 2023 war, the [European Parliament](9581.md) passed a motion stating the need for Hamas to be eliminated, with US president [Joseph R. Biden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joseph_R._Biden) having expressed the same sentiment.[^cite_note-417][^cite_note-418] Hamas was accused of having committed [genocide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allegations_of_genocide_in_the_October_7_attacks) against Israelis on 7 October 2023 by 240 legal experts, including jurists and academics.[^cite_note-419]&#x20;

## Allegations of antisemitism

Further information: [Antisemitism in Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antisemitism_in_Islam) and [Religious antisemitism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religious_antisemitism)

According to critics, Article 7 of the 1988 charter has been interpreted as "openly dedicate(s) Hamas to genocide against the Jewish people".[^cite_note-420] Authors have characterized the violent language against all Jews in the original Hamas charter as [genocidal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genocidal),[^cite_note-421] [incitement to genocide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Incitement_to_genocide),[^cite_note-Gourevitch-422][^cite_note-Goldberg-423] or [antisemitic](1078.md).[^cite_note-424][^cite_note-NAS-425] The charter attributes collective responsibility to Jews, not just Israelis, for various global issues, including both World Wars.[^cite_note-426] *[The American Interest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_American_Interest)* magazine wrote that the charter "echoes" [Nazi propaganda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nazi_propaganda) in claiming that Jews profited during [World War II](32927.md).[^cite_note-Herf-427] [Jeffrey Goldberg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jeffrey_Goldberg), editor-in-chief of *[The Atlantic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Atlantic)* magazine and others have compared statements in the 1988 charter with those that appear in *[The Protocols of the Elders of Zion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Protocols_of_the_Elders_of_Zion)*.[^cite_note-Goldberg-423][^cite_note-428]&#x20;

[Ahmed Yassin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ahmed_Yassin), the founder of Hamas, responded to accusations that "Hamas hate Jews" by stating in a 1988 interview:&#x20;

> We don't hate Jews and fight Jews because they are Jewish. They are a people of faith and we are a people of faith, and we love all people of faith. If my brother, from my own mother and father and my own faith takes my home and expels me from it, I will fight him... So when a Jew takes my home and expels me from it, I will fight him. I don't fight other countries because I want to be at peace with them, I love all people and wish peace for them, even the Jews. The Jews lived with us all of our lives and we never assaulted them, and they held high positions in government and ministries. But if they take my home and make me a refugee like 4 million Palestinians in exile? Who has more right to this land? The Russian immigrant who left this land 2000 years ago or the one who left 40 years ago? We don't hate the Jews, we only ask for them to give us our rights.[^cite_note-429]

Esther Webman of the Project for the Study of Anti-Semitism at [Tel Aviv University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tel_Aviv_University) wrote in 1998 that Hamas leaflets during the [First Intifada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Intifada) "contained the most extreme anti-Semitic statements" of all Palestinian factions, but argued that "anti-Semitism is not the main tenet of Hamas ideology."[^cite_note-430] The tone and casting of the Israeli-Palestinian conflict as part of an eternal struggle between Muslim and Jews by the [Hamas Covenant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1988_Hamas_charter) had become an obstacle for the movement to be able to take part in diplomatic forums involving Western nations.[^cite_note-431]&#x20;

Hamas has promoted [Holocaust denial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holocaust_denial).[^cite_note-DefendingTruth-432]<sup>: 11 </sup>[^cite_note-433] It described [Nazi Germany](21212.md)'s [genocide of European Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Holocaust) as "so-called" and "an alleged and invented story with no basis" in a 2000 press release.[^cite_note-DefendingTruth-432]<sup>: 17–18 </sup> In 2003, Hamas co-founder and deputy leader [Abdel Aziz al-Rantisi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abdel_Aziz_al-Rantisi) said that "Zionists were behind the Nazis' murder of many Jews."[^cite_note-434] In 2009, Hamas was involved in [a dispute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hamas%E2%80%93UNRWA_Holocaust_dispute) with the [United Nations Relief and Works Agency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_Relief_and_Works_Agency) (UNRWA) over the inclusion of [Holocaust education](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holocaust_education) in Gaza, with Hamas's Popular Committees for Refugees describing the Holocaust as "a lie invented by the Zionists".[^cite_note-ReutersUNHolocaust-435]&#x20;

In an [op-ed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Op-ed) in *[The Guardian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Guardian)* in January 2006, [Khaled Meshaal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khaled_Meshaal), the chief of Hamas's political bureau, said that the nature of [Israeli–Palestinian conflict](46216.md) was not religious but political and that Hamas has "no problem with Jews who have not attacked us".[^cite_note-436] In 2008, in another *Guardian* op ed, [Basem Naim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Basem_Naim), Minister of Health and Information in Gaza, rejected the accusation that Hamas is “motivated by anti-Jewish sentiment”. He said that Hamas does not deny the Nazi Holocaust, which he characterises as “one of the most abhorrent crimes in modern history. We condemn it as we condemn every abuse of humanity and all forms of discrimination on the basis of religion, race, gender or nationality.” He also said that Hamas rejects “the exploitation of the Holocaust by the Zionists to justify their crimes”.[^cite_note-Hamas_Condemns_the_Holocaust-305]&#x20;

Hamas' 2017 charter removed the explicitly antisemitic language of the original, stating that their struggle is against [Zionism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zionism) and not Jews, while rejecting persecution or denial of rights of any human being on nationalist, religious, or sectarian grounds.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESeurat201917-56][^cite_note-AlJazeera20170506-59][^cite_note-middleeasteye20170502-437] It also says that the Jewish question, antisemitism, and the persecution of Jews stem from European history rather than Arab or Muslim heritage, while advancing goals for a Palestinian state that many see as consistent with a two-state solution.[^cite_note-Jihad-51][^cite_note-Baconi-108-52][^cite_note-middleeasteye20170502-437] In 2025, Hamas stated that the [Gaza genocide](75668427.md) is "the [Auschwitz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Auschwitz) of the 21st century".[^cite_note-438]&#x20;

## Electoral performance

### Legislative Council

In the [2006 Palestinian legislative election](38945340.md), the party won 44.45% of the vote, becoming the largest party of the [Legislative Council](40067464.md).&#x20;

| Election | Leading candidate | Votes | % | Seats | +/– | Position |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| [2006](38945340.md) | [Ismail Haniyeh](41207463.md) | 440,409 | 44.45 | 74 / 132 | New | **1st** |

## Public opinion

The most recent press release by the People's Company for Polls and Survey Research, dated August 12th 2026, overall support for Hamas has fallen to 24% among Palestinians, Hamas leader Khalil al-Hayya was polling at 16% for [Palestinian President](30580840.md), 20% believe Hamas won the Gaza War while 15% believed Israel won and 60% believe no one won.[^cite_note-439]&#x20;

Following the October 7 attacks and the Gaza war that followed, Hamas's popularity in Gaza fell while increasing in the West Bank. A May 2024 poll by the *Arab World for Research and Development*, a West Bank-based independent organization, only a quarter of Gazans supported Hamas, while 76% of Palestinians in the West Bank views Hamas positively. Views on the attack among Gazans plummeted from 50% support to 24% in favor from a poll taken in November 2023 to the May 2024 poll.[^cite_note-440] According to the poll conducted by *[The Washington Institute for Near East Policy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Washington_Institute_for_Near_East_Policy)* from 14 November to 6 December 2023, 40% of [Saudi](349303.md) participants expressed a positive view of Hamas, 95% of Saudis did not believe that Hamas killed civilians during the October 7 attacks, and only 16% of Saudis said Hamas should accept a [two-state solution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Two-state_solution).[^cite_note-441] A poll conducted by the [Palestinian Center for Policy and Survey Research](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestinian_Center_for_Policy_and_Survey_Research) between 22 and 25 October 2025, found that 70% of Palestinians from the West Bank and Gaza opposed the disarmament of Hamas, even if that means a return to Israeli attacks.[^cite_note-442]&#x20;

A poll conducted in 2021 found that 53% of Palestinians believed Hamas was "most deserving of representing and leading the Palestinian people". Only 14% preferred Abbas's Fatah party.[^cite_note-443] At the same time, a majority of Gazans also saw Hamas as corrupt, but were frightened to criticize the group.[^cite_note-444] Polls conducted in September 2023 found that support for Hamas among Palestinians stood at around 27–31%.[^cite_note-445]&#x20;

In July 2014, 65% of Lebanese viewed Hamas negatively. In Jordan and Egypt, roughly 60% viewed Hamas negatively, and in Turkey, 80% had a negative view of Hamas. In Tunisia, 42% had a negative view of Hamas, while 56% of Bangladeshis and 44% of Indonesians had a negative opinion of Hamas.[^cite_note-pew2014-446] A March/April 2023 poll found that 60% of Jordanians viewed Hamas firing rockets at Israel at least somewhat positively.[^cite_note-447]&#x20;

Public opinions of Hamas deteriorated after it took control of the Gaza Strip in 2007. Prior to the takeover, 62% of Palestinians had held a favorable view of the group, while a third had negative views. According to a 2014 Pew Research survey just prior to the [2014 Israel–Gaza conflict](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014_Israel%E2%80%93Gaza_conflict), only about a third had positive opinions, and more than half viewed Hamas negatively. Furthermore, 68% of Israeli Arabs viewed Hamas negatively.[^cite_note-pew2014-446]&#x20;

## Foreign relations

See also: [Foreign relations of Hamas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_relations_of_Hamas)



Pro-Hamas rally in [Damascus](8914.md), Syria.



Hamas leader [Ismail Haniyeh](41207463.md) and Iranian Supreme Leader [Ali Khamenei](385653.md) in 2012

After winning the Palestinian elections, Hamas leaders made diplomatic tours abroad. In April 2006, [Mahmoud al-Zahar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahmoud_al-Zahar) (then foreign minister) visited Saudi Arabia, Syria, Kuwait, Bahrein, Qatar, the UAE, Yemen, Libya, Algeria, Sudan and Egypt.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESeurat202269-448] In Syria he held talks on the issue of Palestinians stuck on the Syrian-Iraqi border. He stated he met officials from Western Europe in Qatar who did not wish to be named.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESeurat202269-448] In May he visited Indonesia, Malaysia, the Sultanate of Brunei, Pakistan, China, Sri Lanka, Iran,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESeurat202269-448] and participated in the [China–Arab States Cooperation Forum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/China%E2%80%93Arab_States_Cooperation_Forum).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESeurat202270-449] [Ismail Haniyeh](41207463.md) visited Egypt, Syria, Kuwait, Iran, Lebanon, Qatar and Saudi Arabia.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESeurat202271-450]&#x20;

Hamas has always maintained leadership abroad. The movement is deliberately fragmented to ensure Israel cannot kill its leaders.[^cite_note-theatlantic.com-451] Hamas used to be strongly allied with Iran and Syria. Iran gave Hamas an estimated \$13–15 million in 2011 as well as access to long-range missiles. Hamas's political bureau was once located in the Syrian capital Damascus before the Syrian civil war. Relations between Hamas, Iran, and Syria began to turn cold when Hamas refused to back Syrian President [Bashar al-Assad](364813.md); Hamas backed the Sunni rebels fighting against Assad. As a result, Iran cut funding to Hamas, and Iranian ally Hezbollah ordered Hamas members out of Lebanon.[^cite_note-time-452] Hamas was then forced out of Syria, and has tried to mend fences with Iran and Hezbollah.[^cite_note-time-452] Hamas contacted Jordan and Sudan to see if either would open up its borders to its political bureau, but both refused, although they welcomed Hamas members leaving Syria.[^cite_note-al-monitor.com-453]&#x20;

From 2012 to 2013, under the leadership of [Muslim Brotherhood](20742.md) President [Mohamed Morsi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohamed_Morsi), Hamas had the support of Egypt. After Morsi was removed, his successor [Abdul Fattah al-Sisi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abdul_Fattah_al-Sisi) outlawed the Muslim Brotherhood and destroyed the tunnels Hamas built into Egypt. In 2015, Egypt declared Hamas a terrorist organization, but overturned this in June the same year.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESeurat2022108-454] There was a rapprochement between Hamas and Egypt, when a Hamas delegation visited Cairo in 2016.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESeurat2022109-455] Hamas has assisted Egypt in controlling the [insurgency in Sinai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Insurgency_in_Sinai).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESeurat2022109-455] Hamas denied Egypt's request to deploy its own militants in the Sinai leading to tensions between the two.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESeurat2022109-455] Egypt has occasionally served as mediator between Hamas and [Fatah](11104.md), seeking to unify the two. In 2017, Yahya Sinwar visited Cairo and convinced Egypt to open the [Rafah crossing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rafah_crossing), letting in cement and fuel in exchange for Hamas committing to better relations with Fatah; this led to the signing of the [2017 Fatah–Hamas Agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2017_Fatah%E2%80%93Hamas_Agreement).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESeurat2022110-456]&#x20;

The United Arab Emirates has been hostile to Hamas, viewing it as the Palestinian equivalent of the Muslim Brotherhood, a designated terrorist organization in the UAE.[^cite_note-time-452]&#x20;

Hamas enjoyed close relations with Saudi Arabia in its early years.[^cite_note-visit-457] Saudi Arabia funded most of its operations from 2000 to 2004, but reduced its support due to US pressure.[^cite_note-CarnegieEndowment20150901-64] In 2020, many Hamas members in Saudi Arabia were arrested. In 2022, Saudi Arabia began releasing Hamas members from prison. In 2023, [Ismail Haniyeh](41207463.md) visited [Riyadh](51554.md), a sign of improving relations.[^cite_note-visit-457] Haniyeh had long sought to visit, and his requests had been ignored until then.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESeurat202284-85-458]&#x20;

Despite its Sunni Islamist ideology, Hamas has been flexible and pragmatic in its foreign policy, moderating and toning down its religious rhetoric when expedient;[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESeurat2022185-459] it has developed strong ties with Iran,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESeurat2022x-460] and established relations with constitutionally secular states such as Syria and Russia.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESeurat2022x-460][^cite_note-FOOTNOTESeurat2022185-459] [Kyrylo Budanov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kyrylo_Budanov), the chief of Ukraine's [Main Directorate of Intelligence](1406269.md), has accused Russia of supporting Hamas by supplying it with stolen Ukrainian weaponry,[^cite_note-NYT:_UkraineRussia-461] and the [National Resistance Center of Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Resistance_Center_of_Ukraine) alleged that the Russian [Wagner Group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wagner_Group) trained Hamas militants ahead of the 7 October attacks.[^cite_note-Kyiv:_Wagner-462]&#x20;

[North Korean](21255.md) arms were likely used during the 7 Oct assault on Israel.[^cite_note-463] Ali Barakeh, a Hamas official living in Lebanon, claimed the two are allies.[^cite_note-464][^cite_note-465]&#x20;

Hamas leaders reportedly re-established relations with [Kuwait](7515890.md), [Libya](17633.md) and [Oman](22316.md), all of which reportedly have not had warm relations with Fatah.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESeurat2022144-466] The cool relationship between Fatah and Kuwait owed to Arafat's support for [Saddam Hussein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saddam_Hussein) during the [Gulf War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gulf_War).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESeurat2022144-466] This rapprochement was in part due to Hamas' policy of non-interference in the internal affairs of Arab countries.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESeurat2022144-466] [Mahmoud al-Zahar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahmoud_al-Zahar) stated that Hamas does not "play the game" of siding with one Arab nation against another.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESeurat2022145-467] When [Yusuf al-Qaradawi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yusuf_al-Qaradawi), and other Sunni *[ulema](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ulema)*, called for an uprising against Assad's regime in Syria, Mahmoud al-Zahar maintained that taking sides would harm the Palestinian cause.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESeurat2022224-225-468]&#x20;

After the [fall of the Assad regime](78548066.md) in Syria, Hamas congratulated the Syrian people for achieving their "aspirations for freedom and justice," and expressed hope that the [new Syrian government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syrian_caretaker_government) would continue "its historical and pivotal role in supporting the Palestinian people."[^cite_note-469] In 2025, Hamas condemned [Israeli attacks on Syria](78549398.md).[^cite_note-470]&#x20;

### Terrorist designation



&#x20; Designated Hamas as a terrorist organization

&#x20; Designated the military wing of Hamas as a terrorist organization

The United States designated Hamas as a terrorist organisation in 1995, as did Canada in November 2002,[^cite_note-AFPC-NA-471] and the United Kingdom in November 2021.[^cite_note-472] In May 2021, the [Organization of American States](22788.md) designated Hamas as a terrorist organisation.[^cite_note-473] The [European Union](9317.md) so designated Hamas's military wing in 2001 and, under US pressure,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGunning2004234-474] designated Hamas in 2003.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELevitt200650–51-475] Hamas challenged this decision,[^cite_note-476] which was upheld by the [European Court of Justice](87187.md) in July 2017.[^cite_note-Reuters_Court-477] Japan[^cite_note-478] and New Zealand[^cite_note-NZ-r1373-terrorlist-479] have designated the military wing of Hamas as a terrorist organisation.[^cite_note-480] The organisation is banned in Jordan.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELevitt200649-481] Citing previous infiltration attempts in 2018 and 2022, the Philippines declared Hamas as a terrorist organization in 2023.[^cite_note-482][^cite_note-483] In late February 2024, New Zealand re-designated the entire Hamas organisation as a terror entity.[^cite_note-Beehive_29_Feb_2024-484] In December 2024, Switzerland approved a bill to ban the group for five years.[^cite_note-485][^cite_note-486] In April 2025, Paraguay expanded its designation of Hamas as a terrorist organisation to include its political wing.[^cite_note-487] In September 2025, Ecuador designated Hamas as a terrorist organisation,[^cite_note-488] as did Costa Rica on 8 April 2026,[^cite_note-CostaRica-489] and Honduras and Trinidad and Tobago on 15 April.[^cite_note-490][^cite_note-491]&#x20;

In April 2025, Hamas filed a legal case in Britain appealing its designation as a terrorist group. The filing argues that Hamas has never engaged in an armed operation outside of historic Palestine and is not a threat to Western nations. Hamas politician [Mousa Abu Marzook](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mousa_Abu_Marzook) submitted a written statement arguing, "Hamas is not a terrorist group. It is a Palestinian Islamic liberation and resistance movement whose goal is to liberate Palestine and confront the Zionist project."[^cite_note-492][^cite_note-493]&#x20;

Hamas is not regarded as a terrorist organisation by Afghanistan, Algeria, Iran,[^cite_note-books.google.com-494] Russia,[^cite_note-495] Norway,[^cite_note-497] Turkey, China,[^cite_note-498] Egypt, Syria, and Brazil.[^cite_note-Samuel_Feldberg_pp._187-499][^cite_note-500][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAmossy2017273,_n4-501] Many other states, including Russia, China, Syria, Turkey and Iran, have regarded Hamas's (armed) struggle as legitimate.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrenner2017203,_n.27-502]&#x20;

Tobias Buck, a *[Financial Times](136566.md)* writer, wrote in 2012 that Hamas is "listed as a terrorist organisation by Israel, the US and the EU, but few dare to treat it that way", and that in the Arab and Muslim world it had lost its [pariah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pariah_state) status, as its emissaries are welcomed by Islamic governments.[^cite_note-503] In the early 2010s, Hamas was considered a terrorist group by some governments and academics, while others regarded Hamas as a complex organisation with [terrorism](30636.md) as only one component.[^cite_note-504][^cite_note-505]&#x20;

### Israeli policy towards Hamas

See also: [Israeli support for Hamas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israeli_support_for_Hamas)

Historical accounts note that Israel's encouragement of Islamist movements in Palestine long predated Hamas's takeover of Gaza, with Israeli authorities adopting a "divide-and-rule" strategy by supporting conservative and religious groups like the [Palestinian Village Leagues](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestinian_Village_Leagues)[^cite_note-506] and Sheikh Ahmed Yassin's *[al-Mujama al-Islami](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mujama_al-Islamiya)*[^cite_note-507][^cite_note-508] to counter the secular [PLO](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestine_Liberation_Organization). This strategy is documented in statements by several former Israeli figures, including Brigadier General Yitzhak Segev, who admitted to funding *al-Mujama al-Islami* at the request of Israeli authorities;[^cite_note-509] former Civil Administration director [Efraim Sneh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Efraim_Sneh);[^cite_note-510] and former head of the Southern Command [Yitzhak Mordechai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yitzhak_Mordechai),[^cite_note-511] and Israeli professor [Avner Cohen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Avner_Cohen).[^cite_note-512]&#x20;

[Benjamin Netanyahu](56472.md) had been Israel's prime minister for most of the two decades preceding the [Gaza war](74998519.md), and was criticized for having championed a policy of empowering Hamas in Gaza.[^cite_note-TOI123-35][^cite_note-513][^cite_note-514][^cite_note-515] This policy was part of a strategy to sabotage a [two-state solution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Two-state_solution) by confining the Palestinian Authority to the West Bank and weakening it, and to demonstrate to the Israeli public and western governments that Israel has no partner for peace.[^cite_note-CBS11-516][^cite_note-517] This criticism was leveled by several Israeli officials, including former prime minister [Ehud Barak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ehud_Barak), and former head of [Shin Bet](311752.md) security services [Yuval Diskin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yuval_Diskin).[^cite_note-CBS11-516] Saudi Arabia and the Palestinian Authority were also critical of Israel under Netanyahu allowing suitcases of Qatari money to be given to Hamas,[^cite_note-CBS11-516] in exchange for maintaining the ceasefire.[^cite_note-TOI123-35] An op-ed by Tal Schneider for the *[Times of Israel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Times_of_Israel)* reported after the Hamas attack that Netanyahu's policy to treat the Palestinian Authority as a burden and Hamas as an asset had "blown up in our faces".[^cite_note-TOI123-35]&#x20;

### Qatar and Turkey

See also: [Qatari support for Hamas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qatari_support_for_Hamas) and [Turkish support for Hamas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkish_support_for_Hamas)

According to Middle East experts, now Hamas has two firm allies: [Qatar](8238258.md) and [Turkey](11125639.md). Both give Hamas public and financial assistance estimated to be in the hundreds of millions of dollars.[^cite_note-time-452] Qatar has transferred more than \$1.8 billion to Hamas.[^cite_note-allies-518] Shashank Joshi, senior research fellow at the [Royal United Services Institute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_United_Services_Institute), says that "Qatar also hosts Hamas's political bureau which includes Hamas leader Khaled Meshaal." Meshaal also visits Turkey frequently to meet with Turkish Prime Minister [Recep Tayyip Erdoğan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Recep_Tayyip_Erdo%C4%9Fan).[^cite_note-time-452] Erdogan has dedicated himself to breaking Hamas out of its political and economic seclusion. On [US television](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/US_television), Erdogan said in 2012 that "I don't see Hamas as a terror organization. Hamas is a [political party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_party)."[^cite_note-theatlantic.com-451]&#x20;

Qatar has been called Hamas' most important financial backer and foreign ally.[^cite_note-allies-518][^cite_note-519] In 2007, Qatar was, with Turkey, the only country to back Hamas after the group ousted the Palestinian Authority from the Gaza Strip.[^cite_note-time-452] The relationship between Hamas and Qatar strengthened in 2008 and 2009 when Khaled Meshaal was invited to attend the Doha Summit where he was seated next to the then Qatari Emir [Hamad bin Khalifa al-Thani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hamad_bin_Khalifa_al-Thani), who pledged \$250 million to repair the damage caused by Israel in the Israeli war on Gaza.[^cite_note-al-monitor.com-453] These events caused Qatar to become the main player in the "Palestinian issue". Qatar called Gaza's blockade unjust and immoral, which prompted the Hamas government in Gaza, including former Prime Minister [Ismail Haniyeh](41207463.md), to thank Qatar for their "unconditional" support. Qatar then began regularly handing out political, material, humanitarian and charitable support for Hamas.[^cite_note-al-monitor.com-453]&#x20;



Haniyeh (right) with former [Turkish Minister of Culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Culture_and_Tourism_\(Turkey\)) and current [Speaker of the Grand National Assembly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Speaker_of_the_Grand_National_Assembly), [Numan Kurtulmuş](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Numan_Kurtulmu%C5%9F), 20 November 2012

In 2011, U.S. President [Barack Obama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barack_Obama) personally requested that Qatar, one of the U.S.'s most important Arab allies, provide a base for the Hamas leadership. At the time, the U.S. were seeking to establish communications with Hamas and believed that a Hamas office in Qatar would be easier to access than a Hamas bureau in Iran, the group's main backer.[^cite_note-FTQatar-520][^cite_note-SpiegelQatar-521]&#x20;

In 2012, Qatar's former Emir, Hamad bin Khalifa al-Thani, became the first head of state to visit Gaza under Hamas rule. He pledged to raise \$400 million for reconstruction.[^cite_note-522] Sources say that advocating for Hamas is politically beneficial to Turkey and Qatar because the Palestinian cause draws popular support amongst their citizens at home.[^cite_note-523]&#x20;

Speaking in reference to Qatar's support for Hamas, during a 2015 visit to Palestine, Qatari official Mohammad al-Emadi, said Qatar is using the money not to help Hamas but rather the Palestinian people as a whole. He acknowledges that giving to the Palestinian people means using Hamas as the local contact. Emadi said, "You have to support them. You don't like them, don't like them. But they control the country, you know."[^cite_note-524] Some argue that Hamas's relations with Qatar are putting Hamas in an awkward position because Qatar has become part of the regional Arab problem. Hamas says that having contacts with various Arab countries establishes positive relations which will encourage Arab countries to do their duty toward the Palestinians and support their cause by influencing public opinion in the Arab world.[^cite_note-al-monitor.com-453] In March 2015, Hamas has announced its support of the [Saudi Arabian](349303.md)-led [military intervention in Yemen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saudi_Arabian-led_intervention_in_Yemen) against the [Shia](26961.md) [Houthis](17240332.md) and forces loyal to former President [Ali Abdullah Saleh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ali_Abdullah_Saleh).[^cite_note-525] In a controversial deal, Israel's government under [Benjamin Netanyahu](56472.md) supported Qatar's payments to Hamas for many years, in the hope that it would turn Hamas into an effective counterweight to the [Palestinian Authority](24093.md) and prevent the establishment of a [Palestinian state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestinian_state).[^cite_note-CNNDec2023-526][^cite_note-SpiegelQatar-521]&#x20;

In May 2018, [Turkish president](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkish_president) Recep Tayyip Erdoğan [tweeted](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tweeted) to the [Prime Minister of Israel](24898.md) [Benjamin Netanyahu](56472.md) that Hamas is not a terrorist organization but a resistance movement that defends the Palestinian homeland against an occupying power. During that period there were conflicts between Israeli troops and Palestinian protestors in the Gaza Strip, due to the decision of the United States to move [their embassy to Jerusalem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Embassy_of_the_United_States,_Israel).[^cite_note-527] Also in 2018 the [Israel Security Agency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israel_Security_Agency) accused [SADAT International Defense Consultancy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SADAT_International_Defense_Consultancy) (a Turkish [private military company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Private_military_company) with connections to the Turkish government) of transferring funds to Hamas.[^cite_note-528]&#x20;

In February 2020, Hamas leader [Ismail Haniyeh](41207463.md) met with Turkish President Erdoğan.[^cite_note-529] On 26 July 2023, Haniyeh met with Erdoğan and Palestinian Authority President [Mahmoud Abbas](232595.md). Behind the meeting was Turkey's effort to reconcile [Fatah](11104.md) with Hamas.[^cite_note-530] On 7 October 2023, the day of the [attack on Israel](74998317.md), Haniyeh was in [Istanbul](3391396.md), Turkey.[^cite_note-531] On 21 October 2023, Haniyeh spoke with Erdoğan about the latest developments in the [Gaza war](74998519.md) and the current situation in Gaza.[^cite_note-532] On 25 October 2023, Erdoğan said that Hamas was not a terrorist organisation but a liberation group fighting to protect Palestinian lands and people.[^cite_note-Reuters_Erdogan-231]&#x20;

### United States

See also: [Palestine–United States relations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestine%E2%80%93United_States_relations)

In early 2025, the US' [Special Envoy for Hostage Affairs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special_Envoy_for_Hostage_Affairs), [Adam Boehler](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adam_Boehler), talked directly with Hamas in an attempt to secure the release of American hostages. Following these talks, Boehler said that Hamas had proposed a 5 to 10-year ceasefire and a full prisoner exchange, and told [Kan News](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kan_11) that he "does believe" that Hamas would not only lay down its weapons but also leave power in Gaza. He told [Fox News](11121.md) that "Hamas is aiming for a long-term ceasefire. We discussed a cease-fire that includes Hamas disarming, staying out of politics, and ensuring they cannot harm Israel."[^cite_note-ynet20250312-533] Secretary of State [Marco Rubio](5502549.md) told reporters that the talks were a "one-off situation in which our special envoy for hostages, whose job it is to get people released, had an opportunity to talk directly to someone who has control over these people and was given permission and encouraged to do so. He did so."[^cite_note-guardian20250310-534] According to [Ynet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ynet), since he was appointed, Boehler has made several "controversial" proposals, including everything from U.S. commando raids into Gaza "and hinting at the possibility of a long-term cease-fire ("hudna") between Hamas and Israel."[^cite_note-ynet20250312-533]&#x20;

In January 2026, the United States designated three national branches of the [Muslim Brotherhood](20742.md) as terrorist organisations, citing an executive order directing a review of Brotherhood chapters and alleging that the Egyptian and Jordanian branches had provided financial and operational support to Hamas and related violent activities. The designations resulted in sanctions and asset freezes, and were justified by U.S. officials as part of broader efforts to counter networks alleged to support Hamas and similar militant groups.[^cite_note-535][^cite_note-536][^cite_note-537]&#x20;

[^cite_note-:10-33]: &#x20;Higgins, Andrew (24 January 2009). ["How Israel Helped to Spawn Hamas"](https://www.wsj.com/articles/SB123275572295011847). *[The Wall Street Journal](173070.md)*. When Israel first encountered Islamists in Gaza in the 1970s and '80s, they seemed focused on studying the Quran, not on confrontation with Israel. The Israeli government officially recognized a precursor to Hamas called Mujama Al-Islamiya, registering the group as a charity. It allowed Mujama members to set up an Islamic university and build mosques, clubs and schools. Crucially, Israel often stood aside when the Islamists and their secular left-wing Palestinian rivals battled, sometimes violently, for influence in both Gaza and the West Bank. 'When I look back at the chain of events I think we made a mistake,' says David Hacham, who worked in Gaza in the late 1980s and early '90s as an Arab-affairs expert in the Israeli military. 'But at the time nobody thought about the possible results.' Israeli officials who served in Gaza disagree on how much their own actions may have contributed to the rise of Hamas. They blame the group's recent ascent on outsiders, primarily Iran. This view is shared by the Israeli government. 'Hamas in Gaza was built by Iran as a foundation for power, and is backed through funding, through training and through the provision of advanced weapons,' Mr. Olmert said last Saturday. Hamas has denied receiving military assistance from Iran.

[^cite_note-Hroub-60]: &#x20;Hroub, Khaled (2017). ["A Newer Hamas? The Revised Charter"](https://www.jstor.org/stable/26378710). *[Journal of Palestine Studies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Journal_of_Palestine_Studies)*. **46** (4 (184)): 100–111. [doi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doi_\(identifier\)):[10.1525/jps.2017.46.4.100](https://doi.org/10.1525%2Fjps.2017.46.4.100). [ISSN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISSN_\(identifier\)) [0377-919X](https://search.worldcat.org/issn/0377-919X). [JSTOR](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/JSTOR_\(identifier\)) [26378710](https://www.jstor.org/stable/26378710). In a similar vein, Hamas's description of itself appears in a section titled "The Movement" that is couched in language quite different from the 1988 charter. Here, Hamas stresses the nationalist and resistance aspects of its purpose far more than the religious and pan-Islamic ones. ... Framing the struggle in nationalist terms is not only a novel element of the 2017 document but it is repeatedly emphasized and clearly articulated. ... Hamas makes plain that the "conflict is with the Zionist project, not with the Jews because of their religion...The new document offers a definitive framing of the struggle against Zionism and Israel as having nothing to do with religion. ... In "The Position toward Occupation and Political Solutions," the document articulates a stance that reflects the movement's internal consensus on the two-state solution, that is, the creation of a Palestinian state along the 1967 lines. ... The "Resistance and Liberation" portion of the new document is also quite different in its language from the 1988 charter and other past statements. Here, there is a clear assertion of the right to a national liberation struggle on the basis of international law."

[^cite_note-Spoerl_2020_pp._210–244-61]: &#x20;Spoerl, Joseph S. (2020). ["Parallels between Nazi and Islamist Anti-Semitism"](https://www.jstor.org/stable/26870795). *Jewish Political Studies Review*. **31** (1/2). Jerusalem Center for Public Affairs: 210–244. [ISSN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISSN_\(identifier\)) [0792-335X](https://search.worldcat.org/issn/0792-335X). [JSTOR](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/JSTOR_\(identifier\)) [26870795](https://www.jstor.org/stable/26870795). [Archived](https://web.archive.org/web/20240116061115/https://www.jstor.org/stable/26870795) from the original on 16 January 2024. Retrieved 27 January 2024. Strictly speaking, the Hamas Covenant of 1988 focused its anti-Semitic language on Zionists, for example, describing The Protocols of the Elders of Zion as the blueprint for the Zionist project (Article 32) and accusing the Zionists of aiming to "annihilate Islam" (Article 28). The May 2017 "Document" continues in this vein, albeit in somewhat less florid language, asserting that "the Zionist project does not target the Palestinian people alone; it is the enemy of the Arabic and Islamic Ummah posing a grave threat to its security and interests. It is also hostile to the Ummah's aspirations for unity, renaissance, and liberation and has been the major source of its troubles. The Zionist project also poses a danger to international security and peace and to mankind...." (#15). As in the 1988 Covenant, the 2017 "Document" merely takes all the classical tropes of anti-Semitism and focuses them on Zionism, noting that "it is the Zionists who constantly identify Judaism and the Jews with their own colonial project and illegal entity" (#16). In effect, Hamas is saying that it is at war with all Jews except those who are anti-Zionist, thus it is not anti-Semitic. This can hardly be regarded as a serious repudiation of anti-Semitism.

[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEO'Malley2015118-115]: &#x20;[O'Malley 2015](#CITEREFO'Malley2015), p. 118.

[^cite_note-kear-217-116]: &#x20;Kear, Martin (2019). [*Hamas and Palestine: The Contested Road to Statehood*](https://www.routledge.com/Hamas-and-Palestine-The-Contested-Road-to-Statehood/Kear/p/book/9780367584450) (Hardcover). Routledge. p. 217. [ISBN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISBN_\(identifier\)) [9781138585416](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special\:BookSources/9781138585416). Without expressly stating as much, Hamas had agreed to 'respect' UNSC Resolutions 242 and 338, the once reviled Oslo Accords, and by extension, the problematic issue of Israel's existence. While Hamas had previously proposed hudnas with Israel, this was the first time that they had signed any Agreement that tacitly accepted that any future Palestinian state would only consist of the West Bank, the Gaza Strip, and East Jerusalem. After the Agreement, Meshaal reiterated Hamas's position concerning its understanding of what any prospective peace agreement with Israel would look like: that any Palestinian state should be established along the 1967 borders, with East Jerusalem as its capital, acknowledgement of the right of return for all Palestinian refugees, the dismantling of all West Bank settlements, and the complete withdrawal of all vestiges of Israeli rule (Tamimi 2009 : 261; Caridi 2012 : 248). This truncated version of any future Palestinian state was a key ideological concession from Hamas that finally brought it in line with Fatah, and more importantly, with the views of most of the Palestinian public.

All kinds of confusions and conflations between Judaism and Zionism found their way into the Charter, to the disservice of Hamas ever since, as this document has managed to brand it with charges of 'anti-Semitism' and a naïve world-view' Hamas leaders and spokespeople have rarely referred to the Charter or quoted from it, evidence that it has come to be seen as a burden rather than an intellectual platform that embraces the movement's principles."
